A parameter of a declared function whose type is a function type is non-escaping, unless it is `inout` or written `escaping` ([effects.non-escaping-parameters](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.non-escaping-parameters) and [effects.escaping-parameters](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.escaping-parameters)). Its type has an implicit row variable of its own, which the elaborated program writes out: the parameter's row holds it, and so does the function's own row (`FnDecl.ImplicitRows`). A call of the function then raises what its argument raises, through the instantiation of that variable, and only a `catch` whose type the parameter's row names handles it. The function's row variables are the ones it writes, then the implicit ones, in the order a use instantiates them (`FnDecl.rowParams`). A row may hold several variables, and instantiating them joins the rows they stand for (`Row.substRows`). An `escaping` parameter has no implicit variable, and its row is the one written; so is every closure literal's parameter.

A non-escaping parameter may only be called, or given to a non-escaping parameter of the function a call names, alone or in a closure literal that calls it (`Stilt.NoEscape`, E0508). Anything else would let the argument outlive the call that brought its effects. `Counterexamples.Escape` shows what goes wrong: `fn keep(g: fn()) -> fn() { g }` returns its non-escaping parameter, and `main` calls the closure that `keep` returned after the call, outside every row that holds its raise, so a `bool` leaves a `main` that raises nothing.

[effects.no-escape](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.no-escape) makes a row that lacks the implicit variable, where the variable meets it, no error of its own, since only a use that breaks the no-escape rule brings the variable there. The model's checker does the same (`Stilt.Lenient.Typing`). The variable meets a row where the parameter's type meets an expected type: an occurrence of the parameter, or a closure literal that names it, checked against a type. Rows that a program writes never hold the variable, and inferred ones hold it only where the parameter's type has flowed from such an occurrence. So at those checks, and only there, the checker lets the row lack the function's implicit variables (`LenientFits`). Three places keep the variable:

- a call through the parameter performs its row where the call is, and the `catch` rules read it there;
- a closure literal's row holds the effects of its body ([effects.inference](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.inference));
- an argument given to a non-escaping parameter of the function a call names instantiates that parameter's implicit variable with what the argument's row holds beyond the row written, so it is checked as it is (`Lenient.ArgsFit.passed`).

`keep`'s body checks: `g` meets `fn()`, whose row lacks `g'`. So the checker without the no-escape check accepts `escaped`, which is unsound (`typeSoundnessWithoutNoEscape_false`). Typing that checks every row rejects `keep` (`keep_not_typed`).

With the no-escape check, the leniency never applies (`Lenient.HasType.strict`). Each check that it makes lenient sits where the no-escape rule allows neither an occurrence of the parameter nor a closure literal that names one; the rule allows them only as a callee, which is not checked against a type, and as an argument given to a non-escaping parameter, which is checked as it is. So a well-typed program has every row checked (`Program.WellTyped.typedIn`), and `typeSoundness` is proved of that typing (`HasType`). The checker in turn accepts every program whose every row checks (`HasType.lenient`). That is the Reference's reason for the leniency, as a theorem: the variable reaches a row that lacks it only through a use that breaks the no-escape rule.

## Row variables inside a `catch`

[effects.catch-around-row-variables](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.catch-around-row-variables) as it was written allowed a call through a row variable inside a `catch` operand when the callee's row also holds `raise<E>`. A body can form such a row that no caller checks, by an annotation that grows a parameter's row or by instantiating a generic callee's row with the enclosing function's variable. The first two programs of `Stilt.Counterexamples.Paired` do each, and send a `bool` to arms written for a record. [effects.paired-row-variables](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.paired-row-variables) replaces that sentence: the call is allowed only if the enclosing function's signature pairs the variable with the caught type. The model checks this at the call, on the callee's row as the call instantiates it (`CallAllowed.paired`, `FnDecl.pairs`), and doesn't reject rows by themselves.

[effects.paired-row-variables](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.paired-row-variables) still trusts that every caller limits a paired variable to its error type, which [effects.one-error-type](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.one-error-type) does only for a concrete instantiation. A generic caller can give the variable a row that ends in its own variable `r`; the row `{raise<E>, r}` keeps one `raise` label, so [effects.one-error-type](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.one-error-type) accepts it, and the caller's own callers never learn that `r` is limited to `E`. `passedOn` does this and gets stuck as the first two programs do. [effects.passing-to-paired-variables](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.passing-to-paired-variables) requires the enclosing signature to pair `r` with the instantiated `E`. The model checks it at every use of a function, called or taken as a value, closure literals included, since their uses are typed in the enclosing function's scope (`PassesOnPairs`, the last premise of `HasType.fnRef`). [effects.catch-around-row-variables](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.catch-around-row-variables) needs nothing new: a closure body is typed with `catching` off, and each `catch` sets it for its own operand, so a `catch` inside a closure literal is checked like any other.

`RowScope` carries the two relations that the rules read, `pairs` for [effects.catch-around-row-variables](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.catch-around-row-variables) and `passOn` for [effects.passing-to-paired-variables](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.passing-to-paired-variables). `Program.WellTyped` takes both from the signature. `Program.WellTypedWithoutPassing` makes `passOn` hold everywhere, and `Program.WellTypedAsRecorded` makes both hold everywhere, for the proofs that those rules are unsound. Each has the checker's leniency and the no-escape check.

## Accepted programs

A program is accepted (`Program.Accepted`, `Stilt.Accepted`) when it is well typed, which takes in mutability ([modes.what-can-be-modified](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.what-can-be-modified)) and the no-escape check ([effects.no-escape](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.no-escape)), obeys the law of exclusivity ([modes.law](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.law)), and passes the checks of views, `Program.ViewsRead` ([views.change-while-viewed](../reference/13-modes.md#views.change-while-viewed), E0423) and `Program.ViewsContained` ([views.escaping](../reference/13-modes.md#views.escaping), E0422), takes the names of one `when` whose values have the same type as one binding for the law, `Program.HeldNames` ([shared.names-that-may-be-one-place](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.names-that-may-be-one-place)), runs no `when` inside a `when`, `Program.Locks` ([shared.locks](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.locks), E0430), keeps the rules of projections, `Program.Projections` ([projections.once-on-every-path](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.once-on-every-path), E0424, E0425, E0426 and E0427), and of writable bindings, `Program.ViewsWrite` ([projections.reaching-written-source](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.reaching-written-source), E0429, and [projections.writable-bindings](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.writable-bindings), E0428), on which no promise rests ([Views in the calculus](#views-in-the-calculus)), and puts no `fixed` value in a `Shared<T>`, `Program.Demands` ([modes.demands](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.demands), E0432). `soundness` is the one theorem about such a program: at every fuel and on every input, its run keeps each promise of `Program.Promises`. It is sound, as `typeSoundness` says; it never breaks exclusivity, which the model states by what a broken access would change, so in-place mutation, where every `inout` argument is the caller's storage, shows what copy-in, copy-out shows and writes the same output (`inPlaceAgreement`); no view reads a source that changed or is gone, which the model states the same way, so the model of windows, where a view is a window into its source's storage that holds no copy and a view that writes writes its source's storage at once, shows what copies show (`shows_windows`, [Views in the calculus](#views-in-the-calculus)); the reuse of storage that the modes of its bindings allow shows nothing (`modesAgreement`); in the world it ends in, no cell of a `Shared<U>` whose `U` can't reach itself is on a cycle (`Program.WellTyped.cycles`); and no code but the `when` holding a shared value's cell reads or writes it (`Program.keepsHeld`, [Shared values, tasks and parallel calls in the calculus](#shared-values-tasks-and-parallel-calls-in-the-calculus)); and a `when`'s block, from a frame whose function values are lock-free, takes no other cell (`Program.Locks.block`, [Holding no value while waiting](#holding-no-value-while-waiting)). An accepted program also keeps the limits `without panic`, `without overflow`, `without div`, `without alloc` and `without copy` that its functions and `main` carry (`FnDecl.limits`, `Program.limits`), as the Reference infers facts ([facts.inferred-facts](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.inferred-facts) and [facts.without](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.without)): the limited functions are among a set of functions whose bodies hold no site of the fact and name only functions of the set, the greatest of which is the set of functions that the inference finds without the fact (`Program.PanicLimited`, `Program.OverflowLimited`, `Program.DivLimited`, `Stilt.Limits`, and `Program.AllocLimited` and `Program.CopyLimited`, `Stilt.Alloc.Limits`). `soundness` gives the promises of `without panic`, `without overflow`, `without div` and `without alloc` for every call of a limited function and for the run of a limited `main` ([Code without panics](#code-without-panics), [Code that ends](#code-that-ends), [Code without allocation or copies](#code-without-allocation-or-copies)); `without copy` and the rules of in-place storage ([facts.own-storage](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.own-storage), [facts.writes-in-place](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.writes-in-place), [facts.in-place-parameters](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.in-place-parameters), [facts.copy-at-call](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.copy-at-call), [facts.copy](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.copy) and [facts.without-copy](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.without-copy)) are checks of acceptance with no proved run-time promise, while `without alloc` has one.

Of the three things the checks of [Modes and exclusivity](../reference/13-modes.md) keep from happening, the core calculus proves two:

- **Breaking exclusivity**: proved on the core calculus, for every program, by `soundness`. The law reads places and their shapes, of the `inout` arguments of a call and of the place a `for` loop reads ([modes.durations](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.durations)), and so of the places that projections hold, whose forms mean calls ([Projections in the calculus](#projections-in-the-calculus)).
- **A view that outlives its source, or that reads a source while it changes** ([views.escaping](../reference/13-modes.md#views.escaping) and [views.change-while-viewed](../reference/13-modes.md#views.change-while-viewed)): proved on the core calculus, for every program, by `soundness`: with views as windows, a run of an accepted program shows what copies show ([Views in the calculus](#views-in-the-calculus)). A view that writes is a call that takes its source `inout`, so `soundness` covers it as an `inout` argument; `writeAgreement` proves on the calculus of views that write, a block with no parameters, the same of writable views held as windows written through at once, under E0429, E0428, E0423 and E0422 ([Views](#views)).
- **A write through a shared value** ([modes.shared-writes](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.shared-writes)): the model of sharing (`Stilt.Sharing`) shares storage between bindings as a copy of [modes.copies](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.copies) does: a `let` or a `var` of a place, or an assignment of one to the whole of a local, at a copy-on-write type, refers to the place's storage, a modification that the inference (`Stilt.Modes.Uniqueness`, as the facts of `Stilt.Modes.Facts` give it) calls `unique` writes where its binding refers, and one it calls `shared` copies first. The inference walks each binding of a body, every parameter and every name a `let`, a `var`, a `for`, a `catch`, a `when` or a `match` arm binds, from the body's start, along the paths execution takes: a `break` to the end of its loop, a `continue` to its head, a raise to the innermost `catch` or `with` around it, and a loop's head after any number of turns, where a binding a turn gave a copy may hold a copy of any part. A call may store an argument when, by the call's types, a part of it may become a part of the call's result or of a value written through an `inout` parameter ([modes.call-stores](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.call-stores), `Decls.mayStore`), whatever the callee's body does with it, and what it keeps lasts as long as the call's result and as each binding that an `inout` argument of the call is a part of. A `when`'s names are `var` bindings, those of one type may be one place ([modes.shared-when-names](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.shared-when-names)), and the end of a `when` writes each of them, as `Shared.new` writes the binding it takes ([modes.handles](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.handles)). It reads bindings by name, so its verdicts are taken in a program that binds each name once, as the export gives its programs, and in another every binding is shared (`Program.isShared`). `stilt-model` runs it beside the others where a program may share, and the suite's programs agree. `Examples.SharedWrites` gives programs the checker accepts in which a write would reach storage that another binding reads after it, an `inout` parameter that the caller reads when the call returns and a binding assigned a part of its own value, which the inference calls shared ([Theorems](#theorems)), and `Examples.SharedWritesFlow` programs where only the paths execution takes show the copy held. When every binding is shared, sharing agrees with copies for every well-typed program whose places are exclusive (`Sharing.shows_all`), and so does the run of a program that binds a name twice, whose every binding the inference calls shared (`Sharing.Program.runSharing_shows_of_not_nodup`). No theorem yet states it for the inference's verdicts in a program that binds each name once. A closure's call binds what it captures afresh in the model, so a write to a copy of a captured variable never reaches what the closure's next call reads there, and the walk doesn't follow it into closure literals. `modesAgreement` holds whatever the shared writes are.

[cycles.can-form-cycle](../reference/14-tasks.md#cycles.can-form-cycle) lets the collector of cycles skip the values of every `Shared<U>` whose `U` can't reach itself: one that holds neither that same `Shared<U>`, nor a function or handler type, nor a type parameter or a pack. A run-time type holds itself, the types of its fields and variants, as its declaration states them, of its elements, of a map's keys and values, and the `U` of a `Shared<U>`, and what each of those holds (`STy.Holds`). A handle that a value of a type holds leads to a cell of a type whose `Shared` the type holds, unless the type holds a function type, whose closures capture values of any type (`Value.Typed.leads`), and holding is transitive, through the `U` of each `Shared<U>`, so every cell that a chain of cells, each holding a handle to the next, reaches from a cell of type `U` is of a type whose `Shared` `U` holds, unless `U` holds a function type (`World.Typed.chain_leads`). Two types that a run takes for the same differ only in the rows of function types, so they are equal or both hold one (`STy.Eqv.eq_or_fn`). So a cell of a typed world on a cycle is one of a `Shared<U>` whose `U` can reach itself (`on_cycle_can_reach_itself`), and the value of no other `Shared<U>` is ever on one. The fundamental lemma keeps every world of a run typed ([Type soundness](#type-soundness)), so `Program.Promises` states it of the world the run ends in, the one state its outcome carries (`Program.WellTyped.cycles`). A value has no type parameter's type, since a call instantiates them, and the calculus has no pack, no handler type and no `Lazy<T>`; it frees nothing, since no run observes freeing ([cycles.freeing-is-unseen](../reference/14-tasks.md#cycles.freeing-is-unseen)).

## Type soundness

A well-typed program has every row checked (`Program.WellTyped.typedIn`), and the proof is about that typing. `typeSoundness` reads a value's type by what the value does (`Value.Typed`). A run-time type reads a row as every error type it can raise, and the labels of its effects, its `resume` and its `io`, under an environment that gives each row variable the error types and labels it stands for (`STy`, `Ty.close`). A function value has the type its literal or its declaration gives it, read under the environment of the body that made it or of the use that named it, grown as a function value's may be ([effects.function-values](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.function-values)). A closure captures the variables its body uses, which no parameter of it binds (`captureOf`, [expressions.capture](../reference/08-expressions.md#expressions.capture)), and its body is typed in the context of those alone, since typing reads a context only at the variables an expression uses (`HasType.strengthen`, `HasType.captured`).

The invariant is on those environments. A function value's environment composes the uses that led to it, from `main` down, and isn't tied to the call stack: a value made in one body can be called after that body returns. A body's environment keeps its signature's promises (`REnv.Respects`): each variable `v` that the signature pairs with `E` raises only `E` as it reads there. A concrete use gives that by [effects.one-error-type](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.one-error-type), and a use that gives `v` a row holding the enclosing function's `r` gives it by [effects.one-error-type](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.one-error-type) for the row's own label, and by [effects.passing-to-paired-variables](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.passing-to-paired-variables) with the enclosing function's environment for `r` (`REnv.Respects.compose`). A variable that no signature pairs may still stand for two error types. That is harmless: code relies on an error's type only in a `catch` operand, where [effects.catch-around-row-variables](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.catch-around-row-variables) lets a call through a variable only when the signature pairs it, and a function value's run-time type reads its row in full.

The proof is a fundamental lemma, by induction on fuel (`Decls.Typed.sound`): evaluating any instantiation of a well-typed expression, from a frame of its context in a typed world, under an environment that keeps its scope's promises, ends with a value of its type and a frame of the same context, a raise of an error type that its effects allow or a jump that they allow, each with a frame of the same context, a panic, or out of fuel, and never stuck. A jump that its effects allow is a `break` or a `continue` inside a loop's body, and a `return` whose value has its body's result type, which a call ends with that value (`invoke_ok`). It holds for every instantiation because a call runs its callee's body instantiated, and a closure's body as the literal's instantiation left it. It is stated of scopes with no type parameters, those of instances: a call of a generic function instantiates its body's type parameters, by types that the function value carries, and `HasType.substTys` types the instance in the instance's scope, where a bound is met by an impl, which `Trait.m` then finds (`method_ok`).

Rows that a body writes, once instantiated, need not type it. An annotation can pair a variable with an error type that the signature doesn't, wherever no `catch` meets it, and an instantiation can then give the row two `raise` labels, of which the substitution keeps the row's own. So the proof never reads a closure's annotations or a function value's instantiation, which the evaluator keeps but never inspects. `ValueHasType`, which reads them, types the values of `Stilt.Optimality`, which hold no functions. `TypeSoundness` is about the copy-in, copy-out model; on a program that obeys the law of exclusivity, `inPlaceAgreement` gives in-place mutation the same observation.

A world is typed when each of its cells holds a value of the cell's type, read with no row variable (`World.Typed`), and every state the lemma speaks of, a performance's rest resumed in a typed world included, has a typed world beside its frame (`State.Typed`). `Shared.new(v)` at `Shared<U>` puts `v`, of type `U`, in a new cell of type `U` and gives a handle that names it by its type and its place among the cells of that type, so a handle's cell always has the handle's type, and a handle has the type `Shared<U>` whatever world it is read in (`Value.Typed.handle`). A cell's type is read with no environment for row variables, which is why the typing of `Shared.new` takes a `U` whose rows hold none (`Ty.Closed`); calls instantiate type parameters, so a generic body's `U` is ground when it runs.

`main` returns `()` ([program.main](../reference/04-programs.md#program.main)), and no jump escapes it (`Program.WellTyped.not_jumped`), so `TypeSoundness` settles the value that a well-typed program returns: `()`, the only value of that type (`Value.Typed.unit_inv`), which holds no closure. An error that it raises has the type its row allows, read by what the error does, and may hold closures.

## Code without `io`

Printing and reading, `print`, `eprint` and `std.io`'s `write`, `ewrite`, `read_line`, `poll_line` and `read_all`, are the calculus's operations on the world that read or write its output, standard error and input (`Op.io`), and each types only where the row holds `io` (`HasType.op`, E0502): they are natives whose rows hold `io` ([facts.io-and-capabilities](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.io-and-capabilities)). `Shared.new` makes a cell and needs none. A call contributes its callee's row, which fits within the row around it ([effects.missing-effects](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.missing-effects)), so a call of a function whose row holds `io` needs it too. A run-time type keeps a row's `io` as a label beside the effects' and `resume`'s (`STy.io`), which a row variable stands for when its instantiation holds `io`, so a function value called at a type whose row reads as no `io` was made by a closure literal or a declaration whose own row reads as none.

`Decls.Typed.quiet` is a second fundamental lemma, by induction on fuel as `Decls.Typed.sound` is, case by case on the typing derivation: evaluating any instantiation of a well-typed expression whose row reads as no `io`, from a typed state, ends in a world with the output, standard error and input it started with, and the rest of each performance it makes, resumed in a typed world with a value of the type its operation gives, does the same from that world (`Outcome.KeepsIo`). Each form keeps the world when the parts it runs do, from the typed states that `Decls.Typed.sound` gives the parts after the first; a `when` and a `with` change cells alone; and a `with` whose block's row reads as no `io` has clauses whose rows read as none, so the rests it resumes keep the world the clause leaves. `soundness` promises it of every call of a function value whose type's row reads as no `io` (`call_keeps`, `Program.Promises.quietCalls`), and of every run whose `main`'s row holds no `io`, which ends with nothing written and its whole input unread (`Program.WellTyped.quiet`, `Program.Promises.quiet`).

## In-place agreement

`inPlaceAgreement` is a simulation (`Stilt.InPlace.Steps`), which relates the outcomes of the two runs by `Outcome.Rel` (`Stilt.Related`): the same value, raise, jump, panic or performance, each with a frame and a state that agree, and a performance's rests related once resumed with the same value. A copy frame and an in-place state agree when each variable of the frame holds the value its reference reaches in the store, hidden ones included, and the references reach pairwise disjoint parts of the store (`Agrees`). Every step of the evaluator keeps that, and ends with the state evolved only by writes below its references and by new locations (`Evolves`). The store is read as one list value, so that a reference is a path into it and the laws of paths apply.

A call is the one step where the models differ. When it starts, the law of exclusivity makes the places of its `inout` arguments disjoint, an `inout self` receiver among them, whatever their indexes evaluate to (`compat_of_resolved`), so the callee's references are disjoint too. When it ends, the callee has written only below those places and in its own locations. Writes below disjoint paths come to one write to each path of its final part (`Value.setSeq_canon`), and that is copy-in, copy-out's write-back, in the order [values.inout-argument-order](../reference/05-values.md#values.inout-argument-order) gives.

Beyond exclusivity, the proof needs each function and closure to name its parameters once each, as `Decls.WellFormed` and typing require ([expressions.closures](../reference/08-expressions.md#expressions.closures)): copy-in, copy-out writes an argument back from the callee's variable of its parameter's name, which a later parameter of the same name would hide. Both conditions hold of every closure a run makes (`Value.Safe`), and survive the instantiation of a generic function's body.

## Code without panics

[effects.without-panic](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.without-panic) promises that code limited by `without panic` panics only with `overflow` or `deadlock` ([shared.when-on-value-already-held](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.when-on-value-already-held)), and [effects.panicking-operations](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.panicking-operations) names the operations that add `panic`. `Stilt.Panics` states the promise in the core calculus, for each function that the limit covers and for `main`. `Expr.panicFree lim` is the check, under a set of functions that `lim` covers (`Limited`): no name of a function, called or as a value, but one that `lim` covers, nor of a trait's method `t.m` but where `lim` covers every impl's `m` (`Decls.PanicCover`), so that a limited function counts as one that can't panic wherever it is named; no index of a place but a store `xs[e] = v` at an index proved as a read's is, whose value doesn't resize `xs` (`guardedStore`, `storeIndex`), and no index of a value but one proved within the list's bounds; no operation that `Op.panicFree` leaves out, which are `/`, `%`, `div_euclid` and `rem_euclid` but by a divisor that is proved not zero ([std.integers](../reference/17-std.md#std.integers)), slicing a string or a list, inserting into a list or removing from it, `m[k]` on a map but of a key proved there, and the panics written out; and every closure literal's body ([facts.inference](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.inference)), a handler's bodies among them, while a performance and a `with` add nothing of their own ([Effects and handlers in the calculus](#effects-and-handlers-in-the-calculus)), and a `when` adds only `deadlock`, the facts about its names dropped inside its block (`Eval.PanicFree.held`). `Op.apply_calm` and `Op.apply_calm_divisor` prove the classification: every other operation ends with its value, with a panic of the kind `overflow`, or stuck. A divisor is proved when it is written as an integer literal other than zero, or when it is a variable `d` known not zero ([facts.divisors](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.divisors)), an index `xs[e]` when `xs` is a variable and `e` is a variable `j`, `j + k` or `j - k` with `k` a literal, and the facts give `0 <= e` and `e < xs.len()` (`Known.bounds`; [facts.guards](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.guards)), and `m[k]` when `m` is a variable, `k` a variable or a literal, and the map is known to have the key ([facts.keys](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.keys)), which a test of `m.contains(k)` gives, so `m[k]` finds it (`index_calm`). Tests give such facts, `Known` in the model ([facts.guarding-tests](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.guarding-tests)): `d != 0`, `d > 0` or `d < 0`, or the mirror of one, gives its then-branch that `d` isn't zero, `i >= 0`, `i > 0` or `i == 0` that `i` isn't negative, and `i < xs.len()` that `i` is below the length of `xs`, and a test's negation gives the else-branch the facts of the opposite test, so `d == 0` gives it that `d` isn't zero. So does a `for` over a range from an integer literal `a`, to its variable ([facts.known-signs](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.known-signs)): that it isn't zero when `a` is positive, that it isn't negative when `a` isn't, or when `a` is a variable that the facts before the loop know not negative (`Expr.lowFacts`), that it is no less than `a`, and, over `a..xs.len() - c` with a body that doesn't resize `xs`, storing it whole or passing it `inout` whole, that it is below the length of `xs` by more than `c` ([facts.range-loops](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.range-loops)), as each element of the range plus `c` is below the length `xs` had when the loop started, which it keeps (`range_after`, `range_elem`). So `for i in 0..xs.len() - 1 { xs[i + 1] }` and `for i in 1..xs.len() { xs[i - 1] }` are proved: `j + k` reads an element, or overflows computing the index (`eval_offset`, `index`). The negation's fact reaches the code after an `if` too when each path of its then-branch jumps, through sequences, `let` and `var` bodies, both branches of an `if` and every arm of a `match`, ending in `return`, `break`, `continue` or `raise`, as in `if d == 0 { return 0 }` followed by `n / d` (`after_holds`). A condition gives each conjunct of its `&&` chain, read through `!`, and its negation each disjunct's negation of its `||` chain, as the calculus writes them, `if a { b } else { false }` and `if a { true } else { b }`: `Expr.factsAs b` collects the facts that a condition gives when it is `b`, a conjunct's kept only for the variables that the operands after it don't write, and `eval_facts` proves that they hold when it ends with `b`. Since the left operand of `&&` and `||` guards the right one, `d != 0 && n / d > 1` is proved too. The check carries the facts the tests around code give (`Expr.panicFreeIn G`) and drops each one before code that comes after a write of one of its variables, or inside a binding of one of their names: `Expr.writes` names the variables that code assigns or passes `inout`, and `eval_keeps` proves that evaluation keeps every other one. A fact that reads only a list's length, as `i < xs.len()` does of `xs`, survives a store into an element of it: `Expr.resizes` names the variables that code stores or passes `inout` whole, `eval_keeps` proves that every other list keeps its length (`Value.set_sameLen`), and a sequence and a loop's turns drop a fact only when the code before writes a value it reads or resizes a list it reads the length of (`withoutWrites`).

`Decls.PanicCover.call` says that a call of a function the set covers, on arguments whose closures are panic-free and whose named functions are covered, wherever they were made ([facts.calls-through-function-values](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.calls-through-function-values)), from a state whose values are so too, ends as panic-free code may: it panics only with `overflow` or `deadlock`, at every fuel, and so does the rest of every performance it makes. `Program.runCopyWith_panicked` says the same of a run whose `main` is such code, in copy-in, copy-out whatever its order of write-back, and `Program.runInPlace_panicked` carries it to in-place mutation on a well-typed program that obeys the law of exclusivity, through `inPlaceAgreement`; `Program.PanicFree` is the program whose every body is covered. The proof goes by induction on the fuel, as `eval_agrees` does: every value a run makes holds only closures with panic-free bodies and names only covered functions (`Value.PanicFree`), those its world's cells hold included, which a `when` takes out and puts back (`State.PanicFree`), so a call runs a panic-free body, a closure's by its value and a declared function's by the cover, every place a run writes takes only fields, and every index it reads is within its list's bounds.

[effects.without-overflow](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.without-overflow) promises that code limited by `without panic, overflow` panics only with `deadlock`. A set that `lim` covers with `lim.overflows` false holds, besides, no operation that can overflow (`Op.overflowFree`) and no index or store at an index that arithmetic computes, `xs[i + 1]`, and the panic of `Outcome.PanicFree` is then `deadlock` alone. `Op.apply_serene` proves the classification: an operation that `Op.panicFree` and `Op.overflowFree` keep ends with its value or stuck. The calculus's values carry no type, so an operation can't count on its operands fitting their type, and every operation that checks a range is a site of `overflow` in the model: integer arithmetic, `n / 2` included, negation, shifts and methods, and the lengths and counts that give an `i64`, `xs.len()` among them, where [effects.overflow-effect](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.overflow-effect) counts the arithmetic and a few methods; the wrapping operators, comparisons, bitwise operators and floats add none. The model checks `without overflow` only beside `without panic`. `Stilt.Examples.Panics` shows the check at its edges, with `if i >= 0 && i < xs.len() { xs[i] }`, `for i in 0..xs.len() { xs[i] }`, `for i in 0..xs.len() - 1 { xs[i + 1] }`, `for i in 1..xs.len() { xs[i - 1] }` and `for i in 0..xs.len() { xs[i] = xs[i] * 2 }` proved, and neither a test of one bound, `for i in 0..=xs.len()`, `xs[i + 1]` up to `xs.len()`, `xs[i - 1]` from `0` nor `xs[i + k]` of a variable `k`, and `if m.contains(k) { m[k] }` but not after `m = m.remove(k)`: it rejects `let d = 0; 1 / d`, which panics with `divide-by-zero`, and accepts `127 + 1` on `i8`, which overflows, as [effects.without-overflow](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.without-overflow) allows.

The calculus leaves out the key that a store `m[k] = v` or `m.insert(k, v)` gives the code after it, and an `if !m.contains(k)` whose branch stores ([facts.keys](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.keys)): its runs are untyped, and a key its comparison doesn't find again, such as a float, which typing rules out, could be stored. A `let` or a `var` of a variable gives its name the keys that the maps the facts know have the variable's value (`Known.alias`), which [facts.keys](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.keys) doesn't give a binding a program writes: the export binds the key of `m[k] += v` to a temporary before it reads and stores, so `if m.contains(k) { m[k] += 1 }` is proved. It knows signs from tests, from the start of a range loop and from a `let` or a `var` whose initial value is known non-negative, a non-negative literal, a call of `len()`, a variable known not negative, or a `+` or a `*` of those (`Expr.nonnegIn`, `Expr.bindFacts`), and a literal constant is its literal in the calculus ([facts.known-signs](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.known-signs)). It drops a binding's fact at the binding's first write, so it leaves out the accumulators of that paragraph, which a write by `=` or `+=` of a known non-negative value keeps, and the start of a range known non-negative but by a literal or a variable. It leaves out what verification proves of an index against a test of a term, `xs[i + 1]` against `i + 1 < xs.len()`, though a range loop proves one, of a list that isn't a variable, `p.xs[i]`, which the check rejects, the other facts of terms that [facts.terms](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.terms) relates, a store through more than one index, `xs[i][j] = v`, and a then-branch whose path ends in a call of type `Never`, which only typing shows never returns; it drops every fact inside a closure literal and a loop's condition, and keeps a `while` condition's facts for its body alone. A function limited by `without panic` may call a function value that code outside the limit made ([facts.calls-through-function-values](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.calls-through-function-values)), so its promise covers its own code: the calculus states it of calls whose arguments are panic-free.

## Code that ends

[facts.without-div](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.without-div) promises that code limited by `without div` ends, and that code limited by `without panic, div` is total. `Stilt.Ends` states both in the core calculus. `Expr.divFree` is the check of [facts.div](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.div): no `loop`, no `while` but one that counts ([facts.counting-loops](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.counting-loops)), and every call made of a function the program names, of a closure literal where it is written, or through a parameter that isn't marked `escaping`, never of a function value that anything else gives, nor a performance, which calls its handler's clause ([Effects and handlers in the calculus](#effects-and-handlers-in-the-calculus)); a `with` and a `when` add nothing of their own, and a `when` whose block halts halts (`Halts.held`). A call through a non-escaping parameter adds nothing where the body calls it, its sites counted where the argument is written: a function lists the positions of the parameters its body calls through (`H`), each a `let` parameter of a function type with an implicit row variable, which the body starts with the role `halting` (`FnDecl.startRoles`), and each call of the function passes there a closure literal, a function named that asks nothing of its arguments, or a non-escaping parameter of the caller's (`haltingArg`, `Expr.passesHalting`), whose value halts on any arguments (`Value.Halting`, `haltingArg_halts`): a closure literal's body counts in the depth of its text, so the induction that proves a body halts proves the literal's too. The export writes a function at the rows that its non-escaping parameters' arguments bring ([Executable semantics](#executable-semantics)), so the parameters of the functions it writes have no implicit row variable, and in its programs a call through a parameter is still a site: the rule speaks of the calculus's declarations, where a non-escaping parameter keeps its variable. Naming a function adds `div` unless it is one of the set of functions that the check's `lim` covers, so a function limited by `without div` counts as one without `div` wherever it is named. The functions of the set are ranked (`Decls.Ranked`): a body names only functions ranked below its own (`Limited.names`), so no two functions of one recursive group are in the set, and calls its own function only when the call goes down, descending or shrinking as the function's `Descent` says ([Descending recursion](#descending-recursion)); a use of it as a value is a site. `main` may name every function of the set. So a counting loop, and the self-calls of a function that shrink, add no `div`, and a caller that names the function inherits none ([facts.what-proofs-change](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.what-proofs-change)). `Program.divFree` ranks functions by the order of their declarations and tries, for each, the ways of going down that `FnDecl.descents` lists.

A `while` counts (`Expr.countsIn`) when its condition compares a variable, its counter, with a bound made of literals, variables and lengths of lists by `+`, `-` and `*`, by `<`, `<=`, `>` or `>=`, mirrored or not, alone or as the first conjunct of an `&&` chain; its body steps the counter toward the bound by a positive literal, `i = i + 1`, or `i += 1`, which the export writes binding its operand first, `let t = 1; i = i + t` (`eval_letStep`), as a statement of its own block with no `continue` before it (`Expr.contFree`, and `eval_noCont`: such code never ends with one); and neither writes the counter elsewhere nor anything the bound reads. `Decls.Ranked.call` says that a call of a function of a ranked set halts, on arguments that pass function values that halt at the parameters its body calls through (`Passed.HaltAt`), from any state, and `Program.runCopyWith_halts` that a run whose `main` is such code halts, never running out of fuel from some fuel on, in copy-in, copy-out whatever its order of write-back, and `Program.runInPlace_halts` carries it to in-place mutation on a well-typed program that obeys the law of exclusivity; `Program.DivFree.halts` is the program free of `div` whole. Fuel counts nested steps, a `for` loop runs its body at its own fuel, whatever the lists it iterates hold, and a `while` takes one more for each turn, so the fuel a run needs depends on its values: `Halts F P s` says that the runs from `s` reach, from some fuel on, one outcome that doesn't run out (`Stilt.Ends.Halts`). A call halts by induction on its function's rank, then on its measure ([Descending recursion](#descending-recursion)), given that the calls its body makes halt (`CallsHalt`); a body halts by induction on the depth of its text (`Expr.depth`, `eval_halts`), and a counting loop by induction on its counter's distance from its bound, which each turn that goes on brings down (`Halts.whileCounts`): the condition held, the body stepped the counter (`eval_steps`), and the bound kept its value (`Bound.val_congr`). `Program.total_of` adds `typeSoundness` and `Program.runCopyWith_panicked`: a well-typed program whose `main` is free of `panic` and `div` ends, from some fuel on, with `()`, with an error of the type its row allows, or with a panic of the kind `overflow` or `deadlock`, as [facts.without-div](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.without-div) says. `Stilt.Examples.Ends` runs a program without loops but `for`, which ends at fuel 10 and not at 9, and `while i < n { s = s + i; i = i + 1 }`, which ends at fuel 18 with `n = 10` and needs more with `n = 20`, and the same loop stepping by `i += 1`; it rejects a step of `0`, a step inside an `if`, a `continue` before the step, a step away from the bound, and a body that writes the bound.

The calculus leaves out a counting loop's step by a name known positive, and calls through a bound, `Trait.m`, which add no `div` where they are written: the export writes none, but a call of the impl's function at each instance of the generic code. A function limited by `without div` halts on arguments whose values at its non-escaping parameters halt, which is what counting an argument's sites where it is written gives ([facts.div](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.div)); an argument given to such a parameter that the model can't read so, a function value that a binding, a field or a call gives, is a site of `div` where it is passed.

### Descending recursion

[facts.descending-self-calls](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.descending-self-calls) lets a function call itself under `without div` when, at every self-call, the argument at one `let` parameter's position holds a strict part of that parameter, and [facts.shrinking-recursion](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.shrinking-recursion) when every self-call steps one integer parameter, its counter, toward a bound, under a test that shows the counter short of it. A function's `Descent` says which its self-calls do: `.parts k`, on the parameter at `k`, or `.shrinks k grow b`, the counter at `k` going up or down toward the literal or the parameter `b`. The check follows the roles of the body's variables (`Stilt.Ends.Roles`): the descending parameter is `whole`; a tuple, record or variant pattern, with no or-pattern or list pattern in it, that matches a value with a role gives its binders the role `part`, a bare binder keeps the scrutinee's role, and so does a `let` binding its value's, and a field of a value with a role is a `part` (`Expr.roleIn`, `Arm.role`). An arm's guard is code of the arm: it is checked with the roles of its binders, which keep them only when neither the guard nor the body writes one, and with no or-pattern a pattern has one way, so the guard sees the parts the body does. The counter is a `counter`, the parameter its bound is a `limit`, and a test that compares the counter with its bound toward which it goes, by `<` or `<=` up and by `>` or `>=` down, alone, mirrored or as the first conjunct of an `&&` chain, makes the counter `guarded` in the branch where it held, and a comparison whose negation does, in the branch where it failed (`Roles.held`, `Expr.negated`), so `if n <= 0 { 0 } else { count(n - 1) }` shrinks and `if n == 0 { 0 } else { count(n - 1) }` doesn't. A self-call descends when its argument at the descending position is a `part` (`argPart`), and shrinks when its argument at the counter's is `p + s` or `p - s` toward the bound, `p` guarded and `s` a positive literal, and it passes the bound's parameter unchanged (`Descent.calls`). A variable keeps its role only where nothing writes it, a binder with no role hides an outer one, and in a closure literal's body, a loop, a `catch` handler and a `when` block no variable has one.

`Decls.Ranked.call` goes by induction on a call's measure (`Descent.measure`): the size of the value at the descending position (`sizeOf`), since values are finite trees and each part of a record, a tuple or a variant is smaller than it, or the counter's distance from its bound (`countDistance`). Through a body, the roles say what the variables hold against what the body knows of its call (`Meas`, `Roles.Inv`): a `whole` no more than the descending argument, a `part` less, a `counter` and a `limit` the integers the call was passed, and a `guarded` counter short of its bound. Code keeps the variables it doesn't write (`eval_keeps`), a call's frame starts with what its arguments pass (`enter_roles`), an expression with a role gives a value its role describes (`eval_roleIn`), and a test that held shows what its reading compares (`Roles.Inv.held`, `eval_negated`): so a self-call that descends passes a smaller size (`args_descend`), and one that shrinks a counter closer to its bound (`args_shrink`). `Stilt.Counterexamples.Ends` shows that the theorem needs the check: `fn f(n: i64) -> i64 { f(n) }` passes its parameter whole, so no ranking accepts it, and a call of it runs out of every fuel. `Stilt.Examples.Ends` runs the sum of a tree, `count(5)` and `go(0, 3)`, which goes up to `n` under `i < n`, and rejects a self-call on the parameter whole, on a tree the body builds, on a part it writes and on an element of a list, a step away from the bound, and a self-call that changes its bound.

## Code without allocation or copies

[facts.without-alloc](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.without-alloc) and [facts.without-copy](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.without-copy) limit code to no site of `alloc` or of `copy`, its own or one that it inherits. `Stilt.Alloc` states both checks in the core calculus. `Expr.allocFreeIn` finds the sites that make a value ([facts.allocation](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.allocation)): a list literal, a closure literal, an interpolating string and each piece it shows, a record literal of a recursive type and the constructor of a variant with a payload of one, a `with`, a performance, and every native that [facts.native-allocation](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.native-allocation) doesn't name (`Op.allocates`). `Expr.storesIn` walks a body in the order of evaluation and finds the others: a write through an index that isn't in place ([facts.writes-in-place](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.writes-in-place)), an `inout` argument through an index, and an argument that a call copies for an in-place parameter ([facts.copy-at-call](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.copy-at-call)). At each point it knows which bindings hold their own storage ([facts.own-storage](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.own-storage)): a parameter of a function limited by `without alloc`, a binding given a list literal, and one given, at its last use, the storage of a binding that holds its own, the last uses being those that `Expr.markMoves` marks. The in-place parameters are any set closed under the rule of [facts.in-place-parameters](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.in-place-parameters) (`Decls.InPlaceParams`): the least, the Reference's, adds the fewest sites, so a check that some closed set passes is a check that it does. The sites of `copy` are the modifications that the inference of [modes.shared-writes](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.shared-writes) calls shared, in a body and its closure literals' (`ModeEnv.allShared`), and the arguments that a call copies for an in-place parameter ([facts.copy](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.copy)). `Program.AllocLimited` and `Program.CopyLimited` keep each limit as the others are kept: the limited functions are among a set of functions that hold no site and name only functions of the set. Of these rules, only the sites that make a value have a proved run-time promise, `without alloc`'s below: `without copy` and the rules of in-place storage, [facts.own-storage](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.own-storage), [facts.writes-in-place](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.writes-in-place), [facts.in-place-parameters](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.in-place-parameters), [facts.copy-at-call](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.copy-at-call), [facts.copy](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.copy) and [facts.without-copy](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.without-copy), are checks of acceptance alone.

`without alloc`'s promise is stated with a counting evaluator (`Count.eval`, `Stilt.Alloc.Count`): the reference interpreter's evaluator, step for step, with a count of allocations beside its frame that each operation making a value that needs storage adds one to, as it makes it, and that a call carries into its callee and back. `Decls.MakesCover.call` says that a call of a function the set covers, on arguments whose closures make no such value and whose named functions are covered, wherever they were made ([facts.calls-through-function-values](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.calls-through-function-values)), from a state whose values are so too, runs in the counting evaluator as copy-in, copy-out runs it, at every fuel, ending with the count it started with in every state it ends in, and never performs an operation; `Program.runCounting_eq` says the same of a run whose `main` is such code (`Program.AllocsKept`). The proof goes by induction on the fuel (`eval_counts`), as `eval_panicFree` does, through `Eval.Counts`, which each step of the evaluator keeps.

The count leaves out the copies that the other sites stand for: a write through an index that isn't in place, and a call's copy for an in-place parameter. The calculus's values are trees that no two bindings share, so no step of its evaluators copies a list, and the promises that need storage shared between values, that a function limited by `without alloc` copies nothing and updates its in-place parameters in place ([facts.in-place-promise](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.in-place-promise)), and that one limited by `without copy` copies nothing, are left out; the model of sharing (`Stilt.Sharing`), which shares storage, agrees with copies only when every binding is shared ([Accepted programs](#accepted-programs)).

## Moving at a last use

[values.passing-values](../reference/05-values.md#values.passing-values) and [values.consumed-parameters](../reference/05-values.md#values.consumed-parameters) says that passing a value never changes the caller's variable, and that the compiler infers which parameters a function consumes: one that the body returns, stores in a value, rebinds with `var`, or passes to a consumed parameter. When a caller passes its last use of a value, the callee may reuse that memory in place. `MovingAgreement` is the ground for that inference: handing over the storage of a last use, instead of copying its value, can't be observed.

The analysis is syntactic. A read of `x` is a last use when nothing evaluated after it, to the end of its body, mentions `x` (`Expr.movesOk`, [facts.last-use](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.last-use)). An expression mentions the variables it reads, writes or passes of the scopes around it, its free variables (`Expr.mentions`, `Expr.mem_mentions`): a binder's names, in its body, are the bindings it makes, so a later `let x` or a closure literal that binds `x` is no use of an earlier `x`, and a binding declared inside a loop is no use of the next turn. A closure literal mentions what its body mentions but its parameters, so a use inside the literal counts as a use at the literal; those are the variables the literal captures (`Expr.mentions_of_captures`, [expressions.capture](../reference/08-expressions.md#expressions.capture)), and a literal reads, of the variables in scope, only the newest binding of each one it captures (`Frames.capture`, `captureOf`), each of them live, so both models capture the same values. What follows a read is the rest of its expression in the order [values.left-to-right](../reference/05-values.md#values.left-to-right) gives, then the rest of its body: a loop's body is followed by its next iteration, a `catch` operand by the arm, a call's arguments by the roots of its `inout` arguments, which the call uses when it starts, and a body by its `inout` parameters, which go back to the caller. So an `inout` parameter is never a last use. An initializer is followed by its body, where the variable it binds hides any other of that name, so in `var x = move x` the mark is a last use when nothing after the whole `var` mentions `x`, and the new `x` takes the old one's storage. The body of a binder is followed by what follows the binder but the names it binds, so in `{ let x = 2; f(move x) }; g(x)` the mark is the last use of the inner `x`. The analysis marks every argument and initializer that is a variable at its last use (`Program.markMoves`), and the theorem holds for any marking that passes the check, so for every subset of those marks too.

`Moving` is in-place mutation with two changes (`Stilt.Moves.Memory`). `let y = move x` and `var y = move x` make `y` refer to the storage of `x`. A call passes an argument `move x` as the place of `x`, as it passes an `inout` argument: the callee's parameter refers to the caller's storage, the callee changes it where it is, and nothing is written back. Copy-in, copy-out reads `move x` as `x`, copies its value, and passes it as a `let` argument, as the reference interpreter does.

The proof is a simulation, as `inPlaceAgreement`'s is, on the bindings that are still live. A mask records which bindings of a frame are live; a last use ends its binding, whose storage may then belong to another variable or to a callee. The copy frame and the `Moving` state agree on live bindings (`MAgrees`). Each step ends only bindings of variables it mentions that nothing after it mentions, and writes only below the storage of live variables it mentions, in both cases only the newest binding of each name (`Post`, `Kills`, `sel`): so a body ends no binding that one of its binders hides, which what follows the body may still use. That is what a call needs of a last use passed as an argument: nothing after the read mentions the variable, so nothing writes it, and it still holds the value read when the call starts (`MAgrees.held`). The call then starts as in place, with each last use passed as its variable's place, which no other argument overlaps (`compat_handOver`); it ends with copy-in, copy-out's write-back of the `inout` places, and with the end of the variables it handed over, whose storage the callee may have changed (`leave_moved`).

The theorem compares the two models on one program, its marks included. Copy-in, copy-out reads a mark as the variable: `eval` has one case for both, `Memory.bindInit` binds a copy, and `Copy.enter` binds a `moved` argument as a value. A program is checked as its erasure (`Program.erase`), which `Program.markMoves` keeps, so `markMoves_agreement` applies to every well-typed program that obeys the law of exclusivity.

Copy-in, copy-out runs a marked program as the program, up to one thing: a closure's value holds its body, marks included, so the values a run makes carry the marks. `Value.erase` reads every mark in a value's closures as a variable, their captured values included, and `Observation.erase` erases the value that a run returns or raises. Evaluating an expression's erasure, from a frame whose values are erased, in a world whose cells' values are erased (`World.erase`), ends as evaluating the expression does, erased (`eval_erase`): its outcome is related to the other's by `Outcome.erasing`, which relates a value, a raise, a jump, a panic and a performance to their erasures, and the rests of two performances once resumed with a value and its erasure in a world and its erasure, so that the two show the same, erased (`Outcome.Rel.observe_erase`). Reading and writing a place commute with erasure, a call passes each argument erased and a last use as a value, and the body it runs is the erasure of the marked run's body, instantiated alike. So a run of any program shows what a run of its erasure shows, once erased (`Program.runCopy_erase`).

When the erasure is well typed, `main` returns `()`, which holds no closure, so only the error it raises needs erasing: `Observation.eraseRaised` erases the marks of a raised error and leaves every other observation as it is (`Program.runCopy_eraseRaised`). A well-typed program is its marking's erasure, and `Program.runCopy_eq_markMoves_runMoving` joins this to `markMoves_agreement`:

```
(p.runCopy fuel).observe = (p.markMoves.runMoving fuel).observe.eraseRaised
```

for every well-typed `p` that obeys the law of exclusivity and every fuel. The two runs end alike, and if they raise, their errors differ at most in the marks in the bodies of the closures they hold. If the row of `main` has no `raise`, `raiseFree` rules that case out, and the observations are equal (`Program.runCopy_eq_markMoves_runMoving_of_raiseFree`).

Erasing the error is the one limit of the statement, and it comes from what the model leaves out. In the Reference, an error that escapes `main` has a type with `Show`, and a run shows the text that `Show` gives ([program.main](../reference/04-programs.md#program.main) and [program.exit-statuses](../reference/04-programs.md#program.exit-statuses)). A type can hold a function and still have `Show`, through an impl that its home module writes ([traits.structural-impls](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.structural-impls)), so the error itself may hold a closure even there; only its text is shown. The model leaves out `Show`, a trait of the prelude, so it shows the error itself, and can't state that the errors of the two runs give the same text.

## Inferred modes

[modes.bindings-and-axes](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.bindings-and-axes) gives every binding of a body, a `let` or `var` binding, a parameter or a closure literal, five modes: one point on each of the axes locality, uniqueness, once, portability and contention, each ordered from its default (`Stilt.Modes.Lattice`). They are facts, not types ([modes.fact-not-type](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.fact-not-type)): `Stilt.Typing` reads none, and `Program.facts` infers them from the elaborated program. A closure literal that a `let` or `var` binds is that binding, and one that nothing binds has a fact of its own ([modes.bindings-and-axes](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.bindings-and-axes)).

The inference reads the source, where a closure captures the variables it uses ([expressions.capture](../reference/08-expressions.md#expressions.capture)), as the model's closures do when they run (`captureOf`):

- **Locality** ([modes.escaping](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.escaping) and [modes.not-escaping](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.not-escaping), `Stilt.Modes.Escape`). `Expr.leaves` follows each part of a body's value to where it goes, with the part of it that leaves the function: the function's result, a literal that stores it, an assignment to an `inout` parameter, to a part of another binding or through an index, a raise, a parameter that its callee consumes, a closure literal that escapes. An assignment to the whole of another local copies the value and doesn't make it escape. A part counts when its type is copy-on-write (`Decls.cow`), which in the calculus is `str`, `List<T>`, `Map<K, V>`, `Set<T>`, a record with a field of a map type, a function type, a recursive type, or a record, tuple or variant type that holds one. A parameter's locality is its consumption ([values.consumed-parameters](../reference/05-values.md#values.consumed-parameters)), which the same traversal reads with the consumption rule's list of uses instead of [modes.escaping](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.escaping)'s (`paramLocality_escapes_iff`). Consumption is solved over the call graph from no parameter consumed, and reaches its fixed point (`Decls.consumed_fixed`), so a parameter escapes exactly when the program consumes it (`ModeEnv.paramModes_locality`). A callee that names no declared function consumes every argument, which is conservative.
- **Uniqueness** ([modes.copy-on-write-types](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.copy-on-write-types), [modes.copies](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.copies) and [modes.shared-writes](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.shared-writes), `Stilt.Modes.Uniqueness`). `Expr.copies` walks the scope of a `var` binding or an `inout` parameter in the order of evaluation, with the copies of its parts that may still last, each held by the scope of its holder: a binding, a statement or call whose value holds it, or the rest of the function when the holder escapes. An assignment to the whole of another local is a copy, and a copy of a copy is a copy of the binding, held by whatever holds it, so the walk keeps the parts of the binding that each binding in scope holds (`Stilt.Modes.Copies`), each at the place of its value that holds it when that is a field of a record or tuple literal, an element of a list literal or a part assigned to. A value made from a part of such a value, by a field, an index, a `for`'s element or a pattern's binder, holds the copy when its part is the copy's place, a part of the copy when it is inside it, and nothing of the binding when it is beside it, whether the value is bound or assigned. A modification of an overlapping part ([modes.overlap](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlap)) is shared, and so is one inside a loop whose body makes a copy; an assignment of the whole binding is no shared write and ends the copies made before it, and the copies of those, unless its value holds a part of the binding, after which they count as copies of every part. The same walk, which follows which bindings are used after each point, finds the writes the binding makes shared from the other side: a modification that reaches inside a copy of its part that another binding holds, as its value or at the place of a field, an element or a part assigned to, which no assignment to that place has replaced since, while the binding, or another holding a copy of an overlapping part, is used after it (`mirrorsOf`); an `inout` parameter is used after every point, since its value goes back to the caller. A binding is shared where the walk of any binding of its body, or of a parameter, finds it so, wherever in the body that binding is made (`ModeEnv.bodyMirrors`). A value passed to a call that may store it, by the call's types ([modes.call-stores](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.call-stores): a part of it may become a part of the result or of a value written through an `inout` parameter), is a copy, held as the call's result is and by each binding that an `inout` argument is a part of; a binding passed at its last use to a consumed parameter passes on the copies it holds. An operation whose value holds no copy-on-write part, a length, a count, a test or a comparison, copies none of its operands, and every other operation may store each. A `when`'s names are `var` bindings; in the walk of one, another of the same type is the same place, written without replacing it and named where it is named ([modes.shared-when-names](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.shared-when-names)); and the end of the `when` writes each name, shared when a copy of a part of it lasts past the `when`, its value among them, or when the name holds a copy of a binding that may still read it. What a name holds lasts on, held by its handle, and `Shared.new` gives its value to a handle the same way, a shared write of a binding that may still read it after the call, as a parameter's caller may ([modes.handles](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.handles)).
- **Once** ([modes.once-closures](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.once-closures) and [modes.once-parameters](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.once-parameters), `Stilt.Modes.Uses`). `Expr.use` counts the uses of a variable: none, one call outside every loop and closure literal or one pass to a `once` parameter, or more. The `once` parameters are solved over the call graph from every parameter `once`, and reach their fixed point (`Decls.onceParams_fixed`, `ModeEnv.paramModes_once`).
- **Portability and contention** ([modes.fixed-values](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.fixed-values), [modes.contended-values](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.contended-values) and [modes.mode-crossing](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.mode-crossing), `Stilt.Modes.Threads`). A binding is `fixed` when its type holds a view, `View<T>` being the one fixed type of the calculus ([views.fixed](../reference/13-modes.md#views.fixed)), and `contended` when its type holds a `Shared<T>`; a closure literal, bound or not, is either when its type is or when a binding it captures is. A type holds itself and the parts of what it holds, as its declaration states them: a list's and a set's elements, a map's keys and values, a tuple's elements, a record's fields and a variant's payloads at its type arguments, and the arguments themselves, and for a function type the values its calls give back and those they write through its `inout` parameters (`Decls.holds`). A record or variant type reached again holds what its first visit found, so a recursive type that holds neither is neither. A `match` arm's binders carry no type in the calculus, so they take the points of the value matched, which holds what they hold, and the greatest points when the model can't tell that value's. A parameter is `contended` exactly when its type holds a `Shared<T>`, and `fixed` exactly when it holds a view (`ModeEnv.bodyFacts_param`). Each fact sets these two axes (`Modes.threads`), and no reuse reads them; the one check that reads one is the demand of `Shared.new` ([Accepted programs](#accepted-programs)).

`Stilt.Modes.Examples` checks these against the programs of the facts suite (`tests/facts/`), with lists standing for every copy-on-write type.

`ModesAgreement` is the theorem that the rationale of [Modes](../reference/rationale/13-modes.md#modes) asks for: modes change nothing a run observes. Evaluation with every mode at its default is copy-in, copy-out, which reads no mode and copies every value it binds or passes. Evaluation that reuses memory is `Moving` on the program with the marks that the modes allow: `Program.markModes` keeps, of the last uses `Program.markMoves` marks, those of a binding whose modes are `local` and `unique`, the least points of locality and uniqueness (`Modes.reuses`), and, in the body of a closure literal whose modes are `once` (`Modes.runsOnce`), those of its captures, which the closure gives up at its one call as a consumed parameter gives up its argument ([modes.once-parameters](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.once-parameters)). It reads every other mark as the variable (`Expr.gate`), which copies. The context of each expression reads these points from the modes that the facts give each binding (`ModeEnv.bindingModes`, `ModeEnv.paramModes` and `ModeEnv.closureModes`), so the lattice of [modes.bindings-and-axes](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.bindings-and-axes) and the inferred locality, uniqueness and once are what the theorem's reuse reads. A `let` parameter reuses as a `let` binding does; an `inout` parameter's storage is its caller's, and never reuses. The modes the reuse reads keep the thread axes at their defaults (`Modes.default`): no reuse reads portability or contention. The two give the same observation, up to the marks in the closures of a raised error, as for `MovingAgreement` (`modesAgreement`), and exactly when `main` can't raise.

The proof doesn't read the modes. Keeping a subset of a body's marks keeps every check that `MovingAgreement` asks for (`Stilt.Modes.Gate`), so reuse at the marks that any context keeps agrees with copying (`Program.runCopy_eq_markGated_runMoving`). That is a property of the model's memories: they copy a value wherever it is bound, captured or passed without a mark, and count no references, so a last use is all that reuse needs, and the modes only choose which last uses reuse. In particular, a call binds a closure's captures in storage of the callee's own, so the model can't tell a `once` closure's captures given up at its call from a `many` closure's copied; the strategy reuses them only in a `once` closure's body, as `stilt-rc` does. A model with shared storage and reference counts, in which `local`, `unique` and `once` would be what makes reuse safe, is outside the calculus. `InPlaceAgreement` and `pairwiseDisjoint_greatest` are unchanged: the law of exclusivity reads no mode.

## Effects and handlers in the calculus

The calculus holds the effects of [handlers.declaring-effect](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.declaring-effect): an effect declares its operations, each with the type of its argument, its parameters as one value, and of its result, and a row may hold effects beside `io`, `raise<E>` and row variables ([handlers.effects-in-rows](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.effects-in-rows)). `E.op(e)` performs an operation of an effect that its row holds ([handlers.performing-operation](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.performing-operation)): its evaluation ends as a performance, with the state it left and its rest, which, resumed with a value in a world, goes on from that state's frames in that world. Every step of the evaluator passes a performance on with its rest extended: the rest of a sequence, a binding's scope, a call, which a performance leaves as a raise does, writing its `inout` arguments back ([handlers.call-that-handler-ends](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.call-that-handler-ends)), and re-enters when resumed. A handler is a record of closures, one for each operation of its effect under the operation's name, and `with h { block }` runs its block with the handler's effect in its row, handling each performance of that effect that reaches it by calling the operation's closure on the argument ([handlers.with](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.with)). A handler is deep: the rest of the block runs under it again once resumed. A handler that resumes gives the performance its closure's value; one that takes control gives the `with` its closure's value, and `resume(v)` in the closure, a performance of a reserved operation that only the row of such a closure allows, runs the rest of the block and gives the closure the value it ends with ([handlers.resuming](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.resuming)). A closure reaches no place of the code it suspends, its argument a value and its captures copies ([handlers.resuming-keeps-exclusivity](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.resuming-keeps-exclusivity)), so what it ends with comes after the rest of the block, on the frames that rest left, and a closure that never resumes ends the `with` on the frames the performance left (`Outcome.lift`). The world is the closure's: a resuming handler resumes the rest in the world its closure ended in, `resume(v)` runs the rest in the closure's world and the closure goes on in the world the rest left, and whatever the closure ends with, a value, a raise or a panic, keeps the world it wrote.

Type soundness covers them (`Stilt.Soundness.Handlers`): a performance ends as one that its row allows, of the operation's types, whose rest ends soundly once resumed with a value of the operation's result type, and a `with` whose clauses a call where it is may make ends soundly (`Outcome.Ok.handleWith`). `main`'s row holds no effect ([handlers.no-effect-reaches-main](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.no-effect-reaches-main)), so no performance escapes it. The simulations relate two performances by their rests, once resumed with related values, so `inPlaceAgreement`, `movingAgreement`, `eval_erase` and `eval_keeps` cover them, a `with` through `Outcome.Rel.handleWith`. The facts follow [handlers.implicit-effects-and-capabilities](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.implicit-effects-and-capabilities): a handler's bodies are closure literals of the code that writes them, which the checks read as they read every closure literal, and a `with` adds nothing of its own. A performance is a call through a stored function value, so it adds no `panic`, and `Expr.panicFree` accepts it when its argument is panic-free: a panic-free performance passes a panic-free value and goes on, once resumed with one, as panic-free code (`Outcome.PanicFree`), the facts that tests gave before it holding after it, since its rest goes on from the state it left (`Outcome.Reaches`), and a `with` of a panic-free handler calls only panic-free clauses (`Outcome.PanicFree.handleWith`). A performance is a site of `div`, which `Expr.divFree` rejects; a `with` whose handler and block are free of `div` is too, and passes on its block's outcome, which, ending, is no performance (`Halts.handleWith`). So `Program.PanicFree.runCopy` covers programs that perform and handle, and `Program.DivFree.halts` and `Program.total` programs whose `with`s perform nothing, as `Stilt.Examples.Handlers` shows.

A body resumes at most once ([handlers.resume-at-most-once](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.resume-at-most-once)), and only the body of an operation of a handler that takes control names `resume` ([handlers.resume-outside-operation](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.resume-outside-operation)). Typing allows `resume(e)` only under a row that holds `resume`, instantiates no row variable with a row that holds one, and allows a call as code only of a callee whose row holds none (`CallAllowed.resume`), so only a `with` calls such a body. The evaluator makes the rule its own: a call as code of a function value whose row holds `resume` is stuck (`Eval.callAs`), and type soundness proves that no well-typed call is, since a function value's row holds no label its type lacks (`Value.Typed.takesControl_eq_false`). `Expr.resumes` is the check of E0514: the most `resume`s a path through code may run, the sum over the parts of a sequence, a condition and the larger of a branch's arms, an operand and its `catch`'s handler, and two for a loop that holds one; a closure literal runs nothing when it is made. `Expr.oneShot` checks that every closure literal, a handler's bodies among them, resumes at most once when its row holds `resume` and never otherwise, so a `resume` in a closure literal inside an operation's body is E0514, and that every `with` names an effect other than that of `resume`. In a program whose bodies pass the check (`Program.OneShot`), `eval_oneShot` proves, in copy-in, copy-out with any order of write-back, that code that may resume at most once performs at most the `resume`s it counts on each path, every value a run makes holding only checked closures (`Value.OneShot`). So the body of an operation that takes control, as a `with` calls it, performs at most one (`Program.OneShot.clause`), and `runBody`, which runs the rest of the block once for each it performs, runs it at most once on each path: no continuation is resumed twice (`Outcome.OneShot.runBody`). `main` performs none (`Program.OneShot.main`). A call whose `inout` argument is live across a performance is a step that the simulations relate like any other, so `inPlaceAgreement` covers it both ways: resumed, the call goes on and writes its argument back when it ends; ended by a body that never resumes, it writes back what it changed before the performance, as on a raise ([handlers.call-that-handler-ends](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.call-that-handler-ends)). `Stilt.Examples.OneShot` runs both in both models, and shows the check's verdicts.

A handler's clause is called on its operation's argument, a value, from the state the performance left, in which every call it ends has written its `inout` arguments back. A call that passes only values reads nothing of its caller's frames and gives them back, so the clause runs the same from whatever frames the calls it ends left, one outcome for every frame with that frame put back (`callAs_unseen`): whether a place of such a call is written back before or after the clause's body runs can't be observed, and the first code that sees it runs after the `with` ([handlers.when-place-is-seen](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.when-place-is-seen)). `Program.Promises` states it of every program, as a property of the evaluator. A projection's access is a call in the projection's body ([Projections in the calculus](#projections-in-the-calculus)), so a handler that ends the access ends the projection's body with it: the statements after the `yield` are in the performance's rest, which the handler never resumes, and the place it yielded holds what the access wrote before the performance ([handlers.ended-projections](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.ended-projections)).

## Shared values, tasks and parallel calls in the calculus

A program means its sequential run ([tasks.sequential-run](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.sequential-run)): `spawn` runs the task's function to its end before it returns, and every other handler of `async` gives the same output ([tasks.same-output-under-every-handler](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.same-output-under-every-handler)). In that run a shared value is state that every task reaches: a cell of the world, which `Shared.new(v)` makes, holding `v`, and whose handle names it by its type and its place among the cells of that type ([shared.std-sync](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.std-sync); [Type soundness](#type-soundness)). `when a, b { block }` is a form of the calculus, `Expr.held` ([shared.when](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.when)). It evaluates its handles in order, and panics with the kind `deadlock` before it holds any cell if one of them is held ([shared.when-on-value-already-held](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.when-on-value-already-held)); otherwise it holds them all and runs the block with each name a variable of its cell's value.
