A cell always holds a value: one that a `when` holds is marked held (`Cell.held`) and keeps the value it had when taken, or the one last put back, while the block works on its names' variables. However the block ends, with a value, a raise, a jump or a panic, it puts each name's value back in its cell and lets the cell go (`Outcome.release`). A performance that leaves the block puts each value back too, the cell still held, and the rest of the block goes on with its names once resumed. A `with` whose handler drops the rest of its block lets go of every cell that a `when` in that rest holds, each keeping the value put back when the performance left the block (`Outcome.letGo`), so the value keeps what its place held and the next `when` on it holds it ([shared/when_left_by_handler.lt](../conformance/shared/when_left_by_handler.lt)). The calculus's `when` panics with `deadlock` when two of its handles name one cell; the export writes `when a, b` as a test of whether the handles of names of one type are equal, and for each way they may be, the block holding one cell for each set of names whose handles are, the others written as the first of them, which is one place ([shared.names-that-may-be-one-place](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.names-that-may-be-one-place)). The law of exclusivity takes the names of one `when` whose values have the same type as one binding, as that paragraph does: `Program.HeldNames`, a check of `Program.Accepted`, asks the law of each `when`'s block with every name written as the first of its type (`Held.one`). Writing names as coarser ones only makes more places overlap, and a binding hides the same shapes under both when the block binds none of the names (`Expr.exclusive_unmerge`). So the block with any of them written as another of its type obeys the law too (`Program.HeldNames.aliased`), and `soundness` promises it (`Program.Promises.heldNames`): the law that in-place agreement rests on holds of every block the export writes, whichever handles are equal, without trusting how it wrote them. The export names each binding apart, so its blocks bind none of the names of the `when`s around them. A task's handle is the value its function ended with, `t.await()` is `t`, the handlers `task.threads()` and `task.sequential()` are `()`, and a `with` of one is its block, since every task it starts has ended with the block ([tasks.with-waits](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.with-waits)); `async` in a row holds `io` in the calculus, as an effect does. A scope is `()`, `task.scope(f)` is `f(())`, and `s.spawn(f)`, as `task.spawn(f)`, the call of `f` through a `let` binding that holds it, whose raise leaves as any raise ([tasks.scope-failure](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.scope-failure)). `par.map` and `par.each` are `for` loops that call `f` on each element, from the first to the last, `par.both(f, g)` calls `f`, then `g`, and `par.fold` is a function the export writes at each type, which folds the first half of a list and the rest and combines the two with `f`, the tree of [par.fold-tree](../reference/14-tasks.md#par.fold-tree) ([par.sequential-meaning](../reference/14-tasks.md#par.sequential-meaning)).

So `typeSoundness`, `inPlaceAgreement`, `movingAgreement` and the theorems after them cover programs with shared values, tasks, scopes and parallel calls as the export writes them, and the model suite runs them. `soundness` carries the discipline of `when` as its promise `Program.Promises.whenDiscipline`: no code but the `when` holding a cell reads or writes the cell's value. It is a property of the evaluator, which holds of every evaluation, accepted or not, rather than a consequence of the checks, and the calculus runs one task at a time, so it is no statement about threads: no theorem models interleavings. Evaluating any expression of the program, with any fuel, from a state whose world holds a cell, keeps the cell held in every state the outcome carries, a panic's included, and in every state of every rest of a performance resumed in a world that holds it, and keeps every cell in its place, so a handle names one cell throughout (`eval_keepsHeld`): a `when` holds only cells that no one holds, so it puts values back in none held before it, and a `with` lets go only of the cells held since it started. And however a `when`'s block ends, the `when` ends the same way with each name's value at that point put back in its cell and the cell let go, every other cell and the frames as they were (`release_puts_back`), so every access to a held value during the block was an access to its name. The calculus runs one task at a time, as the sequential run does, so two tasks never touch a value at once in it, and what holds among threads is what [tasks.same-output-under-every-handler](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.same-output-under-every-handler) and [shared.when-order](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.when-order) require of an implementation. What the calculus proves toward it is that a call that passes values alone, as a task's function is called, leaves its caller's frame as it was (`Eval.call_at`), and that the calls of `std.par` end alike run each from the state they start from as run in sequence (`parallel_eq_sequential`). The written program obeys the law of exclusivity where the source does: a value a `when` holds lives in a variable of its own, and no other code reaches it while the cell is held ([tasks.values-in-tasks](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.values-in-tasks): tasks reach one another only through the cells of the world and the output, in the order of the sequential run). `Stilt.Examples.Shared` runs `when c, d` with equal handles, a call that reaches a value through its handle, a `when` inside a `when` on one value, which panics with `deadlock`, and a `when` left by a raise, in both models.

Every proof of the calculus covers `when`. Type soundness keeps each cell's value of its type (`held_ok`). The simulations of copy-in, copy-out by in-place mutation and by `Moving` relate states whose worlds are one, their cells holding values that each run may hold: safe ones (`Stilt.InPlace.Holding`), and, for `Moving`, justified ones too, the analysis of last uses keeping a `when`'s names live to the end of its block, where both models read them (`Stilt.Moves.Release`). One-shot handlers keep every value one-shot, those of the cells included (`State.OneShot`). The export declines a lazy value in a program with a `when`, which panics while the lazy value computes ([polymorphism.lazy-waits-for-nothing](../reference/07-types.md#polymorphism.lazy-waits-for-nothing)), where a call of its function doesn't.

`Stilt.Parallel` states what a parallel run of the calls of `std.par` would give, each call run from the same frames, as a thread that copies what it captures runs it, in the one world the threads share and write to, their values in order or the first failure in the sequential order ([par.panics](../reference/14-tasks.md#par.panics)), and proves it equal to the sequential run whenever each call leaves the frames as they were, in the worlds a property holds of, none ends as a performance, and each gives its value in such a world (`Eval.inParallel_eq_sequentially`); for the calls of a typed program, the property is that the world is typed. In copy-in, copy-out, whatever its order of write-back, a call that passes values alone leaves its caller's frame as it was, however it ends (`Eval.call_at`). The closures of `std.par` have empty rows ([par.what-runs-in-parallel](../reference/14-tasks.md#par.what-runs-in-parallel)), and a call of a function value whose type's row reads as no label, on arguments of its types, never ends as a performance (`call_settled`), so `parallel_eq_sequential` holds for every such list of calls: those of `par.map`, of `par.both` and of each level of `par.fold`'s tree. The export calls those closures with values alone, as the theorem does, and runs the calls of `std.par` in sequence, as their meaning is. The same lemma is [tasks.continuations-stay-in-task](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.continuations-stay-in-task): a task's function raises nothing and performs no user-defined effect ([tasks.task-functions](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.task-functions)), so a call of it never ends as a performance (`continuations_stay`).

Left out: threads, and every interleaving of tasks, whose meaning [tasks.same-output-under-every-handler](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.same-output-under-every-handler) defines as the sequential run's ([Shared state and parallel folds](#shared-state-and-parallel-folds) states what holds among threads); `par.each` among the parallel calls, whose calls each write one element of a list; a lazy value in a program with a `when`, whose computation a `when` inside it would have to see ([polymorphism.lazy-waits-for-nothing](../reference/07-types.md#polymorphism.lazy-waits-for-nothing)); and freeing values, of cycles or not, which no run observes ([cycles.freeing-is-unseen](../reference/14-tasks.md#cycles.freeing-is-unseen)).

### Holding no value while waiting

[shared.locks](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.locks) gives a function the inferred fact `locks` when it runs a `when`, inferred as the implicit effects are ([facts.inference](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.inference)). `Stilt.Locks` states it on the core calculus. `Expr.lockFree` says that code holds no site of it: no `when`, no use of a function that has the fact, called or as a value, no method whose impls' functions may have it, and no closure literal whose body holds one, a handler's bodies among them. A call through a function value adds nothing ([facts.calls-through-function-values](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.calls-through-function-values)), and nor do a performance and a `with`. `Decls.locks` is the set of functions that have the fact: the fixed point of the functions whose bodies hold a site, from none, which the export doesn't write. The sets grow, so they settle within one round for each function. The model counts that many rounds and one more, and takes every function as locking if they haven't settled. Either way a function outside the set has a body without a site (`Decls.locks_closed`). E0430 is the check `Program.Locks`, a field of `Program.Accepted`: the block of every `when` the program writes, closure literals' included (`Program.whenBlocks`), holds no site.

`soundness` promises what E0430 is for (`Program.Promises.locks`, from `Program.Locks.block`). In copy-in, copy-out, whatever its order of write-back, with any fuel, the block of every `when`, as written or instantiated, runs from a frame whose function values are lock-free. It holds no cell, in any state it reaches, that its world didn't hold when it started, and the rest of each of its performances holds none that the world it is resumed in doesn't (`Outcome.TakesNone`). A function value is lock-free when it is a closure whose body holds no site or a declared function outside the set, and its parts are lock-free (`Value.LockFree`). Code without a site makes only such values, and a call of one runs a body without a site (`eval_takesNone`). So a task inside a `when` takes no other value before it releases its own, the premise of `Stilt.Shared.Deadlock.Step` under which `no_deadlock` shows that no system of tasks deadlocks ([Shared state and parallel folds](#shared-state-and-parallel-folds)). The link is stated, not proved: the calculus runs one task, and no theorem maps its runs to that model's systems.

The premise on the frame is where calls through function values come in. A function value that a `when` takes out of a cell, or that the code is given, may have been made by code that locks, and calling it inside the block may still take a cell, as [shared.when-on-value-already-held](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.when-on-value-already-held) says, and as the body of a handler between a performance and its `resume` may. `Stilt.Examples.Locks` computes the fact on a few declarations, and checks E0430 on blocks that call a locking function, that hold a closure literal that runs a `when`, and that call a function value.

## Views in the calculus

The calculus holds views as the reference interpreter does: a view is the part it reads, a string or a list, copied when the view is made (the copy of the rationale of [Modes](../reference/rationale/13-modes.md#views)). `View<T>` is a type of the calculus for a viewable `T`, `str` or `List<U>` ([views.viewable-types](../reference/13-modes.md#views.viewable-types)), whose run-time type is `T`'s, so a view's value is typed as its part is. `View.all(x)`, `View.of(x, a, b)` and `v.view(a, b)` are one form of the calculus, `Expr.view`, whose operands are the source and then the offsets, if any: it gives the whole of its source, or the part from `a` up to `b` that `slice` gives, which panics as `slice` does, and has no type unless its source's type is viewable (E0420) or a view type ([views.making-view](../reference/13-modes.md#views.making-view)). The form stands apart from the operations so that a view's source, the place its first operand names when it names one, stays in the program ([views.making-view](../reference/13-modes.md#views.making-view)). `v.copy()` gives the part back as a `T` ([views.functions-of-views](../reference/13-modes.md#views.functions-of-views)), its operand's value. The export writes every other function of a view in those terms: `v.len()`, `v.is_empty()`, `v[i]`, a `for` over `v`, a splice of `v` and `==` on views as the same forms on `v.copy()`, as [views.functions-of-views](../reference/13-modes.md#views.functions-of-views) and [views.for-over-view](../reference/13-modes.md#views.for-over-view) define them. So `typeSoundness`, `inPlaceAgreement` and the theorems after them cover programs with views, and the model suite runs them.

In the models of copies, of in-place mutation and of moving, a view can't tell its source's later changes, and a view that outlives its source still holds its part. The model of windows (`Stilt.Windows`) holds a view as the compiler does, a window into its source's storage that holds no copy ([views.what-view-is](../reference/13-modes.md#views.what-view-is)): it is in-place mutation whose store frees a binding's location when the binding's scope ends ([expressions.scope](../reference/08-expressions.md#expressions.scope)), with `Value.window`, a location, the path to the source within its value and the ranges taken in turn, which only this model makes. A view of a place is a window on the place; of a view, a window on what that view reads; of any other value, a temporary, which nothing can name or change, the part it reads, as in the other models ([views.making-view](../reference/13-modes.md#views.making-view)), so the store of windows has exactly the in-place model's locations. Reading through a window reads its source as it is: an operation, `copy` among them, reads its operands through their windows, as indexing, a field, a `for`'s iterable and a `match`'s scrutinee do ([views.functions-of-views](../reference/13-modes.md#views.functions-of-views)), and so do a performance's argument, a handler's value, a `with`'s value and the values its block raises and returns, which leave the frames whose storage the windows read. A value bound to a binding or passed to a parameter whose type may hold a view, a view type, a function type or a type parameter ([views.view-types](../reference/13-modes.md#views.view-types)), stays as it is, and is read through its windows otherwise; a call's result is read through its windows unless the call's type, which every call carries, is a view type, and so is the error a `catch` arm binds. A view returned or raised stays a window, and `Program.runWindows` shows a raised window as its part. So a window whose source changed shows the change, and one whose source's scope ended is stuck: [views.change-while-viewed](../reference/13-modes.md#views.change-while-viewed) (E0423) and [views.escaping](../reference/13-modes.md#views.escaping) (E0422) are what make a window read what a copy holds. The calculus states both checks on its own syntax, `Program.ViewsRead` (E0423) and `Program.ViewsContained` (E0422), over the sources that `Expr.sources` follows through calls, closures, arms and `catch` arms ([views.making-view](../reference/13-modes.md#views.making-view), [views.across-calls](../reference/13-modes.md#views.across-calls) and [views.fixed](../reference/13-modes.md#views.fixed)), and `Stilt.Examples.ViewChecks` runs them on the required tests; every program of the conformance suite that the export writes passes both. Without either check, a well-typed program tells copies from windows (`Stilt.Counterexamples.Windows`). `stilt-model` runs a program that makes a view with windows too, and finds no program of the conformance suite on which copies and windows disagree. That they agree on every well-typed program that obeys the law and passes both checks is proved, and is a promise of `soundness`: the model of windows simulates in-place mutation (`eval_sim`), so a run with windows shows what one in place shows, and so what copies show (`shows_windows`). The simulation relates a window to the part it reads in place; the reads around an expression guard every live window's region, which a write that E0423 allows misses (`eval_guards` states the same of in-place evaluations alone); a window's sources track where it lies, so a scope's end frees no storage that a live window or a value leaving the scope reads (E0422); and a call reads its `inout` arguments' storage through its windows when it starts and the windows of its value on the callee's storage when it ends. Typing checks no E0421 either ([views.view-types](../reference/13-modes.md#views.view-types)): a type argument may be a view type, and the copy needs no check of where one is stored. Left out of the calculus: `view.get`, `view.chars` and `view.bytes`, and `Iter<T>` with its adapters and consumers ([views.making-iterator](../reference/13-modes.md#views.making-iterator), [views.adapters](../reference/13-modes.md#views.adapters) and [views.consumers](../reference/13-modes.md#views.consumers)). An iterator is a lazy value, whose elements come through its adapters' closures only when a consumer asks for them ([views.laziness](../reference/13-modes.md#views.laziness)), and the calculus has no native type and no value that holds a pending computation but a closure. The calculus can state them only as the code they mean, which the export doesn't write yet: `Iter<T>` as a closure that gives its `n`th element as an `Option<T>`, each adapter a closure over its source's and its own, which asks its source for the elements before the `n`th again, and each consumer a loop that asks for the elements in turn. An adapter's closure has an empty row, so asking again shows nothing but where it panics, at the element a lazy pull first panics at; an iterator of a view is then a closure that captures the view, whose sources it has ([views.fixed](../reference/13-modes.md#views.fixed)), so a binding of it reads them for its scope as [views.reads-that-last](../reference/13-modes.md#views.reads-that-last) asks, and with windows it reads its view when it is made, as every closure does. Such an iterator would have no structural traits, as no function type has ([views.iterator-traits](../reference/13-modes.md#views.iterator-traits)). Projections are forms of the calculus too ([Projections in the calculus](#projections-in-the-calculus)).

A view that writes ([projections.writable-views](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.writable-views)) is a form of the calculus too, `Form.writes` (`Stilt.Writable`), which means the call a writable binding is ([projections.writable-bindings](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.writable-bindings)): `var v = view.into(inout xs, a, b)`, `view.part(inout w, a, b)` of a writable binding `w`, or a call of a function whose result writes, with the rest of its block, is a call of a closure literal whose first parameter takes the binding's source, a list, `inout`, called with the source's place once the offsets are evaluated and checked as `View.of` checks them, a part of a writable binding's counted from its start. The closure's further parameters take the variables the rest modifies, `inout` under their own names, since a closure captures by copy. The binding stands for that parameter and its part's start and end: a read of it is the view of its part, so a view made from it has the parameter as its source, and `v[i]` is a place of the parameter at the index that `Expr.throughOf` gives, `-1` outside the part, so that the access panics with `bounds` where one past a list's end does, after the other operands ([projections.places-through-views](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.places-through-views)). An `inout` parameter of a view type is three parameters, the source's place `inout` and the part's start and end, and so is the argument it takes; a function whose result writes its one `inout` source gives the part's start and end in that argument, and a caller that reads the result reads the view of that part after the call ([projections.results-that-write](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.results-that-write)); a projection's `yield` of a view that writes passes its access the same three; and `view.split(inout xs, at, f)` copies the two parts, which `f` takes as writable bindings of those copies, and writes both back into `xs` when `f` returns or raises. So in copies a writable binding writes a copy of its source, written back when its scope ends, however it ends, and in place and with windows it writes the source's storage at once ([projections.what-view-writes](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.what-view-writes)): `inPlaceAgreement` and `shows_windows` cover it as they cover any `inout` argument, and the law of exclusivity is what makes them agree.

`Program.ViewsWrite`, a check of `Program.Accepted`, states the rules of writable bindings on these forms: the scope's closure reaches no variable its source is rooted in, read or written ([projections.reaching-written-source](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.reaching-written-source), E0429), and no `var` binding has a view type unless it is a writable binding's form ([projections.writable-bindings](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.writable-bindings), E0428). No promise rests on it: the closure reaches every other variable by copy, so the models agree whatever it reaches. E0429 is what makes that copy what the compiler reads in place, and `Stilt.Counterexamples.Writes` shows, in the calculus of views that write, the run that tells a window from a copy without it ([Views that write](#views-that-write)). `Stilt.Examples.Writable` runs these forms in the three models and checks the rules on them, and every program of the conformance suite that the export writes passes the check. Left out: a view assigned to the whole of a writable binding, a jump out of a writable binding's scope, a `yield` or a `resume` in it, a writable binding of a map's element, a scope that writes another part of its source's root, the view that a read of a projection's call of a view type copies out, a function whose result writes as a value, and one whose result writes when it has more than one `inout` source or its result isn't a view made through `inout` of that source.

## Projections in the calculus

A projection ([projections.declaring-projection](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.declaring-projection)) is a form of the calculus, which it writes as the calls it means, the way the export writes it, so every model of memory runs it and every theorem above covers it (`Stilt.Projections`). The caller's access to a projection's call is a closure literal of the caller's, which takes the yielded place `inout` and does with it what the caller does with the place the call is, and the projection is a function of its own for each type of access, whose last parameter takes the access. Its body is a form, `Form.projection` (`Expr.projectionBody`): the list it keeps the access's values in, empty, the body the projection was declared with, and the first value kept, which the function gives. Its `yield p` is a form, `Form.yield` (`Expr.yieldOf`), which calls the access with `p` `inout`, and the places the access threads, and adds the access's value to the list; a `return` in the body gives the value kept, `Form.yielded`, so the body has type `()` and its `return` takes no value ([projections.yield](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.yield)). A call of a projection where a place is is a form, `Form.project` (`Expr.projectCall`), the call with its arguments and then the access ([projections.call-is-place](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.call-is-place)). Typing checks each form as the calls it means (`HasType.form`). The projection's arguments, the other operands of the call or the assignment, and the indexes of the place are evaluated first, in their order, into temporaries; the projection's call then starts where the access does, runs its body to its `yield`, where the access happens on the yielded place, resumes after it, and writes its `inout` arguments back when it ends ([projections.when-projection-runs](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.when-projection-runs)). An access that raises leaves it without resuming, as a raise leaves any call, its `inout` arguments holding what was written before ([projections.raising-during-access](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.raising-during-access)), and so does a performance whose handler ends the access ([handlers.ended-projections](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.ended-projections)). A closure captures by copy, so the other places that the access's call takes `inout`, and the places that projections before it in the call yield, go through the projection as `inout` arguments of its own; the projections that are arguments of one call start from left to right, each inside the one before it, and resume from right to left. A read of a projection's call is the access that copies the yielded place out.

`Program.Projections`, a check of `Program.Accepted`, states the rules of projections on these forms (`Stilt.Projections.Check`). `Expr.once` follows the paths through a projection's body ([projections.once-on-every-path](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.once-on-every-path), E0424): a `yield` is a statement, so the paths go through the statements of the body's blocks, the branches of an `if`, the arms of a `match` and the handler of a `catch`, a `return`, a `raise` and a panic end theirs, and any other code runs no `yield` (`Expr.keeps`), so one in a loop, a guard, the operand of a `catch` or a closure literal is rejected, as are a second `yield` on a path and a path to the end or to a `return` that has run none. Code that runs no `yield` writes no list kept, never names the access, and ends with no `break` or `continue` of a loop around it, nor with a `return` before the `yield` (`Expr.exits`). A `yield` and the value it keeps are only in a projection's body ([projections.yield](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.yield), E0425); the body raises nothing of its own, no `raise` and no call marked by a `try` outside the operand of a `catch`, since its row holds no `raise`: what it raises is its access's ([projections.cannot-raise](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.cannot-raise), E0427); and a projection is named only as the callee of a call where a place is ([projections.not-a-value](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.not-a-value), E0426). The operand of a `yield` may be a projection's call, whose own access calls the access once ([projections.nested](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.nested), `Expr.accessOnce`).

What the check promises is a theorem of `soundness` (`Program.Projections.yields`): the body of every projection of an accepted program, run in copy-in, copy-out with any fuel from where its list is empty, ends with exactly one value kept on every path to its end or to a `return`, and with at most one on every path to a raise or a performance, whose rest, resumed with any value in any world, again ends so, and with no `break` or `continue`. So the value its function ends with is there, and the caller's access ran once. Code that runs no `yield` keeps every variable it doesn't write (`eval_keeps`), the list among them, and ends with no jump that `Expr.exits` excludes (`eval_exits`), so its paths run the `yield`s their start had (`keeps_yielding`); a `yield` reads the list, calls the access, which keeps it, and stores it with the access's value added (`yield_yielding`); and the rest follows each statement, branch and arm (`eval_yielding`).

The law of exclusivity of the written program is the source's: two projections' calls that hold one source, as in `swap(inout g.at(0, 0), inout g.at(1, 1))`, become a call that takes that source `inout` twice, which it rejects (`Stilt.Examples.Projections`, which also runs projections in both models and checks the rules on bodies that keep and break them). Every program of the conformance suite that the export writes passes `Program.projectionsChecked`. Left out: a projection's call as an argument of a projection's call (the rest of [projections.nested](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.nested)), a trait's projection called through a bound, an access that passes a row parameter of the caller's on or raises an error other than the projection's own, and a `yield` of a view that isn't a place, but for one that `view.into`, `view.part` or a call whose result writes makes.

A view that writes, a writable binding of one, `view.split` and a `yield` of a view that writes are in the calculus as calls that take their source `inout` ([Views in the calculus](#views-in-the-calculus)).

## Views

[views.what-view-is](../reference/13-modes.md#views.what-view-is) adds views, windows into part of a value that someone else owns ([views.what-view-is](../reference/13-modes.md#views.what-view-is)). The rationale of [Modes](../reference/rationale/13-modes.md#views) asks the model for view bindings as reads that last their scope, and for the theorem that under [views.change-while-viewed](../reference/13-modes.md#views.change-while-viewed) a view's window and a copy of the viewed part agree. Views are windows in a calculus of their own too (`Stilt.Views`), whose one viewable type is a list ([views.viewable-types](../reference/13-modes.md#views.viewable-types)): the part of the core calculus that a view meets, which is `let` and `var`, places with fields and indexes, assignment, list and record literals, field access, indexing, `if`, `for` and blocks, with `View.of`, `v.view(a, b)`, `v.len()`, `v[i]` through a view and `v.copy()` ([views.making-view](../reference/13-modes.md#views.making-view) and [views.functions-of-views](../reference/13-modes.md#views.functions-of-views)). It reuses the core calculus's values, paths, shapes, `hide` and `unread`, and leaves that calculus and every theorem above as they were. A program is the body of one function, whose `let` parameters hold the caller's arguments in storage that outlives the call ([views.across-calls](../reference/13-modes.md#views.across-calls)).

The two models of a view are those of the rationale of [Modes](../reference/rationale/13-modes.md#views). A **window**, the compiler's, is the location of its source's storage, the path to the viewed list within it, and two offsets, and reading through it reads that storage as it is now. A **copy**, the reference interpreter's, is the viewed part, copied when the view is made. The models share everything else: a variable that holds a value has a location of its own, which its scope's end frees ([expressions.scope](../reference/08-expressions.md#expressions.scope)), and one that holds a view holds the view itself, which no value can hold ([views.view-types](../reference/13-modes.md#views.view-types): no type of the calculus holds a view type, so E0421 can't arise). A window whose source is gone, freed or shorter than its end, is stuck: it reads storage that a compiler can't tell from what was there.

A view's **sources** ([views.making-view](../reference/13-modes.md#views.making-view)) are the shape of the place it was made from, those of the view it is a part of, or none for a temporary, which nothing can name or change (`VExpr.sources`). The law of exclusivity of `Stilt.Exclusivity` extends to them (`VExpr.exclusive`). A binding of a view reads its sources for its scope, as a `for` loop reads its place for its body, and so does the view that a `for` iterates ([views.reads-that-last](../reference/13-modes.md#views.reads-that-last) and [views.for-over-view](../reference/13-modes.md#views.for-over-view)). A view that no binding holds reads them while the expression that holds it still runs: while the index of `v[i]` and the offsets of `v.view(a, b)` are evaluated. A modification of a place that overlaps any of these reads is E0423 ([views.change-while-viewed](../reference/13-modes.md#views.change-while-viewed)): the check of E0404, against more reads. A new binding hides the reads of its name, as in [modes.law](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.law). With `loops`, a `for` over a list reads its place too, and E0404 is checked; without it, only the reads of views are. [views.escaping](../reference/13-modes.md#views.escaping) is `VExpr.contained` (E0422): the rest of a block, whose value the block gives, reads no binding that the block makes. A function's body is a block, so a view of a local can't be returned, and a view of a `let` parameter can.

`viewAgreement` is a simulation, as `inPlaceAgreement` is. The two states have the same store, and environments that bind the same names to the same locations, or to a window and a copy that agree: the window reads, in the store, the part the copy holds (`StateAgrees`). A window into a location is **guarded** by the reads when every variable in scope that names the location names it by a shape among them (`Guarded`). A write that the law allows is to a place whose shape overlaps none of the reads, so it reaches a part disjoint from every guarded window's, whatever its indexes (`write_source`). Every window that a binding in scope holds is guarded (`InScope`), and so is a view that an operand is evaluated under, since its sources are among the operand's reads (`Post.held`). A view's sources **cover** its window when every binding of its location, hidden or not, names it by one of them (`Covered`), which survives the scopes that end around the view. When a scope ends, the value of the rest of the block reads none of the storage it frees, since [views.escaping](../reference/13-modes.md#views.escaping) keeps the binding's name out of that value's sources (`Closed.of_post`). Evaluation keeps the simulation at every fuel (`simulates`), and the theorem follows from the state a body starts in.

The theorem holds of every program that obeys the law with the reads of views and [views.escaping](../reference/13-modes.md#views.escaping), typed or not (`viewAgreement_reads`), so E0404 isn't among its hypotheses: a loop over a list iterates the value the list had when it started, as copy-on-write makes it, which a change to the list in the body leaves as it was (`viewRelisted_agrees`). E0423 and E0422 can't be dropped: on the core calculus, `viewsRead_needed` and `viewsContained_needed` give a well-typed program each that copies and windows tell apart. On `viewShrunk` the window reads past the end of its source's storage, so E0423 is what keeps a view from reading moved or freed storage: a soundness rule, as the Reference says, where E0404 is a cost rule.

### Views that write

The core calculus holds a view that writes as a call that takes its source `inout` ([Views in the calculus](#views-in-the-calculus)), whose agreement in every model is that of an `inout` argument. The calculus of this section holds it as a window written through at once beside a copy of its part written back, and is where E0429 is shown to be needed.

[projections.writable-views](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.writable-views) and [projections.what-view-writes](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.what-view-writes) lets a view write its source, and the rationale of [Modes](../reference/rationale/13-modes.md#writable-views) asks the model for writable views, the evaluator that writes through them, and the theorem that under [projections.what-view-writes](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.what-view-writes) a window and a copy written back when the binding's scope ends agree. `Stilt.Writes` is a calculus of its own for them, as `Stilt.Views` is for [views.what-view-is](../reference/13-modes.md#views.what-view-is): the part of the views calculus that writable views meet, which is `let` and `var`, places that are a variable followed by indexes, assignment, list literals and indexing, with `View.of`, `view.into(inout xs, a, b)` ([projections.writable-views](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.writable-views)), `v.view(a, b)` and `v.len()`, `var` bindings of a view type, which are writable ([projections.writable-bindings](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.writable-bindings)), and `v[i] = e` through them ([projections.places-through-views](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.places-through-views)). It reuses the views calculus's types, store and models, and leaves that calculus and `viewAgreement` as they were. A program is a block with no parameters. Projections and `yield` ([projections.declaring-projection](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.declaring-projection) and [projections.yield](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.yield)) need calls, which neither calculus of views has; the core calculus has them as the export writes them ([Projections in the calculus](#projections-in-the-calculus)).

The two models are those of the rationale of [Modes](../reference/rationale/13-modes.md#writable-views). In the window model, the compiler's, every view is a window, and a write through a writable binding writes its source's storage at once. In the copy model every view is a copy: `view.into` copies the part with the place it was made from, a writable binding writes its copy, and the end of its scope writes the copy back into that place, as copy-in, copy-out writes an `inout` argument back. A binding that doesn't write holds such a view as the part alone, since it reads ([projections.what-view-writes](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.what-view-writes)).

A writable binding holds what its view writes as writes for its scope (`WExpr.exclusive`), and any access to them, a read among them, is E0429. Any other binding, and a view that no binding holds, holds its sources as reads, whatever its view writes, so `let w = view.into(inout xs, 0, 1)` reads `xs`. A view made from a writable binding has the binding as its source ([projections.places-through-views](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.places-through-views)), so a write through the binding while that view lives is E0423; the model adds what the binding writes to those sources, which E0429 keeps from every access for the binding's scope already, so it rejects nothing more but where a block hides one of those names. E0428 (`WExpr.bound`) accepts a `var` binding of a view type only when its view writes every source it has, which in the calculus is a view that `view.into` made, as a block's last expression or not. Typing makes `v[i]` a place only through a writable binding, and gives `view.into` a place that the body can modify, as an `inout` argument.

`writeAgreement` is a simulation, as `viewAgreement` is. The two stores have the same locations and the same values, except where a writable binding writes: there the window's storage is what the copy's would be with the binding's part written back (`Restored`). E0429 keeps it so: every variable that names that location is among the writes, so no read sees the copy's storage and no write lands under the part (`InScope.locked`), and no two writable bindings write one location (`InScope.apart`). A write through a writable binding writes the window's storage and the copy's part alike (`restore_put`), and the end of its scope writes the copy back, which then gives the window's storage. A read-only window keeps reading its part while every binding in scope that can change its location, by holding it or by writing it through a window, is frozen by the reads and writes in progress (`Guarded`), and a view's sources cover every binding that can change its location (`Cover`), as in the views calculus.

`Stilt.Counterexamples.Writes` shows what the theorem needs. Without E0429, `let w = View.of(xs, 0, 3)` while `var v = view.into(inout xs, 0, 3)` lives passes every other check, and `v[0] = 9` reaches the value that `w` holds: `w[0]` is 9 through a window and 1 through a copy (`writeLocks_needed`). That is the record's ground for calling E0429 a soundness rule: a write through a view would change a value that another binding holds ([values.value-semantics](../reference/05-values.md#values.value-semantics)). Without E0423 on a view made from `v`, `let w = v.view(0, 2)` does the same (`writeViewed_runs`), and without E0428, `var v = View.of(xs, 0, 3); v[0] = 9` writes `xs` through a window and nothing through a copy (`bound_needed`). A list bound by `let ys = xs` before the view is made keeps its value in both models (`writeShared_runs`): a binding holds a value of its own, as copy-on-write gives it once `view.into` makes the storage unique ([projections.writable-views](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.writable-views)).

### Guards and list patterns

[patterns.guards](../reference/09-patterns.md#patterns.guards) tries an arm's guard with each way its pattern matches: an or-pattern's alternatives from left to right, and of several, the leftmost one's varying slowest, so a guard may run more than once, with its effects each time. The core calculus has guards and constant patterns ([The calculus](#the-calculus)). `Stilt.Guards` states them on a calculus of its own too, with the patterns the core calculus leaves out, `[p₁, …, pₙ]`, `[p₁, …, pₙ, ..]` and `[p₁, …, pₙ, ..rest]`, beside constants ([patterns.constants](../reference/09-patterns.md#patterns.constants)), over integers, Booleans, `()`, tuples, lists and `Option`, and expressions that read, add, compare and print, so that a run shows each time a guard runs. `Pat.ways` lists every way a pattern matches a value, each as the bindings it makes, and a `match` tries its arms in order, taking an arm without a guard by its first way and trying a guard with each way until it is true.

`tryWays_first` and `select_first` say that the arm taken is the first, and its way the first, whose guard is true, every earlier one tried and false. `check` is the usefulness algorithm of [patterns.algorithm](../reference/09-patterns.md#patterns.algorithm) over these types, a list column split into the lengths up to its longest pattern's and the lists longer, as that many elements and the list after them; it never sees an arm with a guard or a constant ([patterns.guards-and-constants](../reference/09-patterns.md#patterns.guards-and-constants)), and it runs on fuel, saying no when the fuel runs out. `check_sound` says that a check that says yes means that every vector of values of the column types matches a row, whatever the fuel, so `run_sound`: a well-typed `match` that the check accepts ends with a value of its arms' type and never falls through. `check_complete` is the converse: when every vector of values of the types matches a row and no row holds a constant, a check whose fuel exceeds the rows' cost says yes, so `exhaustive_complete`: arms without a guard or a constant that cover every value of a type are found exhaustive. The cost of a column (`Stilt.Guards.Measure`) is 1 for a column of wildcards and otherwise exceeds the costs of the columns that each of its constructors' rows give, the longest list pattern bounding a list column's lengths, so each step costs less than the rows it comes from: the list after the elements of the longer lists comes back as a column of wildcards, whose cost is 1. A column whose patterns are among another's costs no more (`cost_mono`), which is what lets a constructor's rows be measured by the constructor's parts of the first column alone. It rests too on each constructor's rows keeping every row that matches a value the constructor builds (`SpecFull`), on every type having a value, so that a column of wildcards loses none, and on an integer that no literal of the column names, which only wildcards match. `run_ok` says that without the check it still never goes wrong, and `let_sound` and `for_sound` that an irrefutable pattern ([patterns.irrefutable-positions](../reference/09-patterns.md#patterns.irrefutable-positions)) in a `let` or a `for` head binds every value of its type. `Stilt.Guards.Examples` runs both examples of [patterns.guards](../reference/09-patterns.md#patterns.guards), the second printing the four pairs the guard sees in their order, and shows the check rejecting the arms `x if x > 0` and `x if x <= 0` and arms that name a value through a constant. The calculus leaves out records and the program's variant types, which the core calculus has, a guard's effects other than printing, the check's warnings of unreachable arms, and the duplicate names that E0208 rejects, which soundness doesn't need: a way's bindings are looked up first to last.

### Trait parameters and kinds

[traits.trait-parameters](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.trait-parameters) has a trait's parameters fixed by its impl's type ([traits.trait-arguments](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.trait-arguments)), so that a type still has one impl of each trait, and names them through a type as member types, such as `C.Item` ([traits.member-types](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.member-types)). `Stilt.Traits` states what that buys on a calculus of types of its own: named constructors, type parameters, curried application and member types. `Normal` replaces each member type of a type headed by a named constructor by the argument its impl gives, at any depth, and leaves one of a type parameter abstract, as the checker does until a call instantiates the parameter. Under coherence (`Coherent`, [traits.coherence](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.coherence)) a member type has at most one normal form (`normalize_deterministic`), and `Stilt.Counterexamples.Traits` shows the theorem needs it: parameters that `Self` doesn't determine, as Rust's `From<T>` has, allow `Convert<f64>` and `Convert<str>` both for `i64`, and `i64`'s member type then normalizes to either.

A kind is the number of arguments a constructor still takes, and a type has kind 0 ([traits.constructor-traits](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.constructor-traits), [traits.constructor-impls](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.constructor-impls) and [traits.constructor-parameters](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.constructor-parameters)). Application is curried, so a constructor applied to some of its arguments has a kind but is no type, and partial application has no source form. `kindOf_subst` proves that a call's instantiation of a constructor parameter by a constructor of its arity, which the head of the argument's type gives, keeps the kind of every type. A type applied to an argument and a constructor given as one are ill kinded (`app_of_type`, `app_of_ctor`), and `map_for_f` shows why an impl of a constructor trait for a constructor of another arity is E0337: with `Map` for `F`, `F<i64>` is no type.

The calculus leaves out inference, bounds and calls themselves: it states the two facts that the checker's dispatch on member types and its instantiation of constructor parameters rest on. The core calculus has member types as elaboration declares them: `C.Item` is a type parameter of its function named `C.Item`, which the bound of `C` whose trait has the parameter `Item` gives as that parameter's argument, so each use gives it the argument that the impl for the type of `C` gives, as `TypeArgsFit` asks of every bound's arguments, and exactly one bound of `C` has such a trait, or it is E0334 or E0335 (`Decls.MemberTypes`).

### Rigid type parameters

[polymorphism.rigid-types](../reference/07-types.md#polymorphism.rigid-types) checks an argument given where a polymorphic function type `fn<T>(…) -> …` is expected with `T` a rigid type, equal only to itself, and each use of the argument instantiates `T` afresh ([polymorphism.instantiation](../reference/07-types.md#polymorphism.instantiation)). `Stilt.Poly` states why checking once covers every use, on a calculus of its own: `i64`, type parameters and function types, and variables, integer literals, closures whose parameter's type is written, as the expected type writes it, and calls. `HasType.substTy` proves that replacing a type parameter by any type keeps a typing, and `Accepts.instance` that an argument accepted with `T` rigid, in a scope that names no `T`, has at each use the type that instantiating `T` gives. `Stilt.Counterexamples.Rigid` shows why the parameter must be rigid: `|x| 0` where `fn<T>(T) -> T` is expected is rejected (`constZero_rejected`, E0311); read with `T` fixed to `i64` it has type `i64 → i64`, and a use with `T` a function type would get a closure that returns an `i64` where a function is expected (`constZero_unsound`).

The calculus leaves out bounds, effects and inference: it states the fact that the checker's rigid types rest on. The core calculus checks a generic function's body the same way, with its type parameters rigid and its bounds, and proves that the instances keep the typing (`HasType.substTys`).

### Fresh types

[meta.fresh-types](../reference/16-meta.md#meta.fresh-types) gives the field, the element or the payload that an arm or a loop body reads a fresh type, a rigid type of that arm, and E0351 keeps a value whose type holds one from leaving it. An arm is then a closure whose parameter has the fresh type, accepted where `fn<F>(F) -> τ` is expected, and each run gives `F` the type the value has. `Stilt.Poly.Fresh` states, in the calculus of [Rigid type parameters](#rigid-type-parameters), that a term has at most one type (`HasType.unique`), that an arm whose `τ` doesn't name its fresh type has type `τ` on every run (`FreshArm.run`), and that an arm whose value is what it reads has type `i64` on an integer and a function type on a function, so no type outside the arm covers its value (`leaking_has_no_type`).

### Record bounds

[records.record-bounds](../reference/07-types.md#records.record-bounds) lets a type parameter carry a record bound, `P: { f: T, .. }`, met by a declared or anonymous record that has each field it names with exactly that type, and a body reads `p.f` through it. `Stilt.Records` states why checking the body once covers every record a call gives it, on a calculus of its own: `i64`, `bool`, type parameters and records whose fields have `i64` or `bool`, and variables and reads of fields, from a record that has the field or through a parameter's bound. `HasType.inst` proves that replacing a type parameter by a type that meets its bound keeps a typing, and `bound_read` that the read `p.f` has the bound's field type at every such type, as `b.v` does at `Box<i64>` and at `{ tag: bool, v: i64 }` (`value_at_box`, `value_at_tagged`).

The calculus leaves out fields of other types, updates, inference and the checks of the bound at a call (E0318): it states the fact that a read through a bound rests on.

### Spreads over packs

[variadics.spread-expressions](../reference/07-types.md#variadics.spread-expressions) and [variadics.spread-checks](../reference/07-types.md#variadics.spread-checks) gives a body no way to take a pack apart, only to map over it with a spread, whose body is checked once, with the pack's element a type of its own ([variadics.spread-checks](../reference/07-types.md#variadics.spread-checks)). `Stilt.Packs` states why that covers every pack, on a calculus of a spread's body: `i64`, `elem`, the pack's element, and lists of those, and `x`, the pack tuple's element, integer literals and one-element lists. `HasType.eval` proves that the body, evaluated at an element of type `σ` at `a`, has its type at `a`, and `spread_map` that mapping it over a pack tuple of `n` elements gives `n` values whose types are the body's type at each element in turn, which is the spread's type at the pack ([variadics.type-spreads](../reference/07-types.md#variadics.type-spreads)), the empty pack included ([variadics.empty-pack](../reference/07-types.md#variadics.empty-pack)). `Stilt.Counterexamples.Packs` shows why a body can't index a pack tuple ([variadics.pack-tuples](../reference/07-types.md#variadics.pack-tuples), E0342): `xs.0` has the type `i64` at `Ts = (i64,)` and none at the empty pack (`first_at_none`), so no type is its type at every pack (`first_ill_typed`).

The calculus leaves out several packs, packs of packs, bounds, effects and inference: it states the fact that checking a spread once rests on.

### Hygienic expansion

[macros.hygiene](../reference/16-meta.md#macros.hygiene) makes a pattern macro's expansion hygienic: a name that its template binds is fresh, its other names resolve where the macro is declared, and a fragment's tokens resolve where the use is. `Stilt.Macros` states what that buys, on a calculus of its own: integer literals, names, sums and `let`, whose names carry the mark of the expansion that wrote them, `0` for the user's, as the reference interpreter marks `tmp#1` ([macros.hygiene](../reference/16-meta.md#macros.hygiene)). A template has the same forms over unmarked names, with fragments `$i`, and its expansion numbered `k` marks each name it writes with `k` and puts each argument in its fragment's place ([macros.expansion](../reference/16-meta.md#macros.expansion)). `hygiene` proves that when no argument reads a name marked `k`, the expansion means what the template means, with its names read from the scope the marks stand for, where the macro is declared, extended by its own binders, and each fragment evaluated in the user's scope. So the template's binders capture none of the user's names, and which names the user picks changes nothing. `withTmp_hygienic` runs `with_tmp!(tmp + 1)`, whose template binds `tmp`, and gets the user's `tmp` plus one, and `withTmp_captures` shows why the mark must be fresh: expanded with the user's mark, the template's `tmp` captures the fragment's.

The calculus leaves out patterns, repetitions, nested uses and the resolution of names in modules: it states the fact that the marks rest on.

### Shared state and parallel folds

[shared.when-order](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.when-order) and [shared.locks](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.locks) make a `when` wait until it holds every value it names, all at once, and reject a `when` whose block runs another (E0430). `Stilt.Shared.Deadlock` models a system of tasks, each with the `when`s it has still to run, each the list of the values it names, and the values it holds inside one. A step either takes all the values of a task's next `when` at once, when no task holds any of them, or releases what a task holds, which is the only step of a task inside a `when`, since its block runs no other. `no_deadlock` proves that while some task hasn't ended, some task can step: a task inside a `when` can release, and when none is inside one every value is free, so a task with a `when` left takes all it names. So no system deadlocks. The premise that a task inside a `when` acquires nothing is a promise of `soundness` in the sequential run of the core calculus, for code that calls no function value made by code that locks ([Holding no value while waiting](#holding-no-value-while-waiting)).

[par.fold-tree](../reference/14-tasks.md#par.fold-tree) makes `par.fold` combine a list in a balanced tree that depends on its length alone. `Stilt.Shared.Fold` defines that tree, `shape n`, as a function of the length, with `shape_split` proving it is [par.fold-tree](../reference/14-tasks.md#par.fold-tree)'s, the first `⌊n/2⌋` elements and then the rest, and `shape_size` that it combines `n` elements. `tree` fills it with a list's elements, and `fold` evaluates it in order, left half first. A schedule is any sequence of `Step`s, each combining two values anywhere in the tree, so halves run in any interleaving. `scheduled` proves that every schedule that ends gives `fold`'s value, which therefore depends on the list alone, and `schedule_progress` that a schedule never gets stuck before it ends. The examples evaluate `10 * p + q`, which isn't associative, over three and four elements, as [par.fold-tree](../reference/14-tasks.md#par.fold-tree) writes them.

[handlers.call-that-handler-ends](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.call-that-handler-ends), [handlers.ended-projections](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.ended-projections) and [handlers.when-place-is-seen](../reference/12-effects.md#handlers.when-place-is-seen) to [tasks.continuations-stay-in-task](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.continuations-stay-in-task) settle four interactions. `Stilt.Interactions.Ended` models a call whose body writes its `inout` places and performs operations, which the handler resumes or ends the call at: copying the places in and writing them back however the call ends gives the store that writing in place gives (`ended_agree`), and leaving out the write-back when the handler ends the call doesn't (`unwritten_differ`). `Stilt.Interactions.Resumed` models a handler's body that resumes once: given a value, it writes no place of the code it suspends (`values_untouched`), while an `inout` place written after `resume` leaves different stores in place and copied (`inout_differ`), which agree only when nothing is written after it. `Stilt.Interactions.Held` gives each name of a `when` the value it reaches and that value's type: places that [shared.names-that-may-be-one-place](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.names-that-may-be-one-place) accepts reach different places of the store even when two names reach one value (`accepted_disjoint`), so copies agree with writes in place (`accepted_agree`), as they don't without the rule (`aliased_differ`), which `Program.HeldNames.aliased` states on the core calculus ([Shared values, tasks and parallel calls in the calculus](#shared-values-tasks-and-parallel-calls-in-the-calculus)); and a `when` that enters only when none of its values is held keeps every value held by one running `when` at most (`enter_keeps`, `leave_keeps`). The fourth, [tasks.continuations-stay-in-task](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.continuations-stay-in-task), the core calculus states: a call of a task's function never ends as a performance (`continuations_stay`, [Shared values, tasks and parallel calls in the calculus](#shared-values-tasks-and-parallel-calls-in-the-calculus)). The models leave out nested calls, paths longer than one field, and threads themselves, which [tasks.continuations-stay-in-task](../reference/14-tasks.md#tasks.continuations-stay-in-task) reduces to the rows of tasks. The core calculus states the second and the third of its own runs too: a handler that ends a projection's access ends the projection's body, and a handler's clause runs the same from whatever frames the calls it ends left (`callAs_unseen`, [Effects and handlers in the calculus](#effects-and-handlers-in-the-calculus)).

The models leave out the values themselves, the order of [shared.when-order](../reference/14-tasks.md#shared.when-order) among the blocks of one value, panics and the other functions of `std.par`, whose meaning is their sequential run, which the core calculus runs ([Shared values, tasks and parallel calls in the calculus](#shared-values-tasks-and-parallel-calls-in-the-calculus)): they state the facts that deadlock freedom and deterministic folds rest on.

## Optimality of the call rule

The call rule of [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) rejects a call with two overlapping `inout` arguments, or with an `inout` argument that overlaps an `inout self` receiver (E0402). `Stilt.Optimality` proves that no sound rule of its kind accepts more.

A call rule (`CallRule`) judges a call by the shapes of its `inout` arguments, in order, a method call's `inout self` receiver first, as [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) does: the place of each, with every index removed ([modes.overlap](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlap)). `Expr.exclusiveWith rule` is the exclusivity pass with `rule` in place of [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout)'s, and the check for E0404 kept. A call rule is sound (`CallRule.Sound`) when every well-typed program that obeys that pass gives the same observation under copy-in, copy-out and in place, with every fuel. [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout)'s call rule is `pairwiseDisjoint`, and the pass of `Stilt.Exclusivity` is the pass with it (`Expr.exclusive_eq_exclusiveWith`), so `inPlaceAgreement` makes it sound. `callRule_le_pairwiseDisjoint` proves that a sound call rule accepts only lists of shapes that `pairwiseDisjoint` accepts. So [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) is the greatest sound call rule (`pairwiseDisjoint_greatest`), and a call rule is sound exactly when it accepts nothing that [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) rejects (`callRule_sound_iff`).

The proof takes a list of shapes `ss` that [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) rejects, so with two overlapping shapes: `h = ss[a]`, and `d = ss[b]` with the same root, whose fields are `h`'s followed by `extra`. A rule that accepts `ss` accepts this program, `Optimality.witness`:

```
fn witness(inout p0: T…, inout p1: T…, …) {
  p_b.v = 1
  let r: i64 = [0][p_a.extra.v]
}
var x: T_K = …   // one for each root of ss, with every i64 field 0
witness(inout ss[0], inout ss[1], …)
```

Each argument is its shape's place with fields only, so the call's `inout` shapes are exactly `ss`, in order. The program has no loop, so E0404 has nothing to reject. In place, `p_a.extra.v` and `p_b.v` are the same part of `main`'s store, so the read sees 1, and indexing the one-element list `[0]` with it panics with the kind `bounds` ([effects.panic-kinds](../reference/12-effects.md#effects.panic-kinds); [std.lists](../reference/17-std.md#std.lists)). Under copy-in, copy-out, `p_a` holds a copy of its place taken when the call started, the read sees 0, and the run returns `()`. `main` returns `()`, as [program.main](../reference/04-programs.md#program.main) requires, and a run shows a panic with its kind, so the two runs show different observations.

Records are nominal and a literal gives every field, so no record type can contain itself. The witness types its places with one record type for each budget `b` up to `K`, the most fields of a shape of `ss`. The type with budget `b` has a field `v: i64`, whose name no shape uses, and, when `b > 0`, a field of the type with budget `b - 1` for each field name of `ss`. Each root is a `var` of the type with budget `K`, and each parameter has the type its place reaches. The proofs type and run this program for every such `ss`, where `Stilt.Counterexamples.Aliasing` runs one fixed program, `alias(inout a, inout a)`.

The two runs differ inside the callee. In place, the body panics, and the panic ends the call, which nothing can observe an `inout` argument after ([values.inout-arguments](../reference/05-values.md#values.inout-arguments)). Under copy-in, copy-out, the body returns before any place is written back, and then every write-back succeeds, whatever was written back before it, since each value has its place's type. So the bound doesn't rest on the order that [values.inout-argument-order](../reference/05-values.md#values.inout-argument-order) gives the write-back. `Copy.memoryWith w` is copy-in, copy-out with that order as a parameter: a `WriteBack` gives, for the parameters that each call binds, a permutation of them, which may depend on their names and on the places they name. With [values.inout-argument-order](../reference/05-values.md#values.inout-argument-order)'s order, from left to right, it is `Copy.memory` (`Copy.memoryWith_leftToRight`), the model of `Program.runCopy` and of every theorem but these. A call rule is sound under `w` (`CallRule.SoundWith`) when the two models agree, with copy-out in the order `w`, on every well-typed program that obeys the pass with it. The witness returns `()` in every order (`copy_runWith`), so a rule sound under any one order accepts only what [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) accepts (`callRule_le_pairwiseDisjoint_anyOrder`), and is then sound under [values.inout-argument-order](../reference/05-values.md#values.inout-argument-order)'s order (`CallRule.SoundWith.sound`). That [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) is sound under the other orders isn't proved: `inPlaceAgreement` is about [values.inout-argument-order](../reference/05-values.md#values.inout-argument-order)'s.

The theorem compares rules that see shapes, as [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) does. Among them, [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) loses nothing. A rule that saw more could do better: `swap(inout xs[0], inout xs[1])` passes two places of shape `xs`, so [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) rejects it, but they are disjoint. `Optimality.indexed` is such a call, `alias(inout xs[0], inout xs[1])`, where `alias` is the witness's body for two places of type `i64`, `x = 1; let r: i64 = [0][y]`. `y` never sees the write to `x`, and both models return `()` on it (`indexed_runs`). A rule that saw the values of indexes could accept it.

Without indexes, [modes.overlap](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlap) removes nothing. A program is index-free (`Program.IndexFree`) when no place that it reads, assigns or passes `inout`, in any of its bodies, holds an index; it may still index a value that is no place, as the witness's `[0][…]` does. On such programs a place is its shape, so a rule that saw whole places would see no more there than shapes. A call rule is sound on them (`CallRule.SoundIndexFree`) when the two models agree on every well-typed index-free program that obeys the pass with it, which asks less than `CallRule.Sound`. The witness's places hold no index (`witness_indexFree`), so a rule sound on index-free programs still accepts only what [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) accepts (`callRule_le_pairwiseDisjoint_indexFree`), and a rule is sound on them exactly when it is sound (`CallRule.soundIndexFree_iff`). `indexed` holds indexes (`indexed_not_indexFree`), so it is outside that class.

E0404 is outside the theorem. The pass keeps it unchanged, and the witness has no loop, so the theorem is about the call rule alone. In the model, a `for` loop iterates the value its iterable had when the loop started, in both models of memory, as copy-on-write makes it do ([modes.change-while-iterating](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.change-while-iterating)). So E0404 rules out a hidden copy rather than a difference in meaning, and its necessity isn't a question of agreement. No theorem here says whether the models would still agree without it.

## Methods and receivers

[traits.impl-blocks](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.impl-blocks) and [traits.methods](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.methods) declares methods in the `impl` blocks of a type, as functions whose first parameter is `self` or `inout self`. The model has those blocks for records and variant types (`ImplDecl`), without type parameters or bounds. Trait impls are blocks of their own (`TraitImpl`), whose functions are named `Trait for Type.m`, which no source name can be. A function of a block is a function of the program under its qualified name `Type.m` (`Decls.fns`), as [traits.qualified-calls](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.qualified-calls) names it. `Decls.Receivers` puts `self` where [traits.methods](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.methods) does: only as the first parameter of a function of a block, of the block's type. `self` is a keyword ([lexical.keywords](../reference/02-lexical.md#lexical.keywords)), so no other binder can take its name; the model has no lexical structure, and checks this of parameters only.

A method call `recv.m(args)` is the call `Type.m(recv, args)` (`Expr.methodCall`): the receiver is the first argument, passed in the mode of `self`. That is the meaning the Reference gives a receiver:

- `Type.m` is a function value whose first parameter is the receiver, and `Type.m(x, …)` calls the method that `x.m(…)` calls ([traits.method-resolution](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.method-resolution) and [traits.qualified-calls](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.qualified-calls)). So `e.bump()` and `Counter.bump(inout e)` are one call.
- A call evaluates its arguments, receiver included, from left to right, and only then starts ([modes.durations](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.durations)). The receiver is written first, so its indexes are evaluated first, its place is read when the call starts, and it is written back first, the order [values.inout-argument-order](../reference/05-values.md#values.inout-argument-order) gives `inout` arguments. `Stilt.Examples` runs the first two: `cs[raise 1].get(raise 2)` raises 1, and `c.get(c.reset())` sees the change that the argument makes to the receiver, as `xs.push(xs.pop() else 0)` does in the Reference. The order of write-back shows only where places overlap, which [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) rejects.
- The receiver of an `inout self` method is a modification that lasts for the whole call, as an `inout` argument is, and a receiver taken by `self` is a read that ends once it is evaluated, before the call starts, which passes an independent copy, as a `let` argument does ([modes.kinds-of-access](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.kinds-of-access) and [modes.durations](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.durations); [values.passing-values](../reference/05-values.md#values.passing-values)). The first must be a place, though no `inout` marks it ([expressions.inout-arguments](../reference/08-expressions.md#expressions.inout-arguments)), that can be modified ([modes.what-can-be-modified](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.what-can-be-modified)), and a well-typed method call has one (`HasType.inoutReceiver`).

The reference interpreter elaborates a method call to the same call, with the receiver as its first argument in the mode of `self`.

So a method call's `inout` shapes start with its receiver's, and [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout)'s two clauses at one call, two overlapping `inout` arguments or an `inout` argument that overlaps an `inout self` receiver, are one check: no two of the call's modifications overlap (`Expr.exclusive_inoutReceiver`). A method call is a call, so every theorem above covers it as stated: `typeSoundness`, `raiseFree`, `inPlaceAgreement`, `movingAgreement`, `Program.runCopy_eq_markMoves_runMoving`, `pairwiseDisjoint_greatest` and `callRule_sound_iff` quantify over programs, whose declarations hold `impl` blocks and whose calls include method calls. In particular, a sound call rule compares the receiver with every `inout` argument (`callRule_receiver`), and `Counterexamples.Receiver` shows it on the required test of [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout): `a.f(inout a.b)`, where `f` takes `inout self` and returns `self.b` after setting `b` to 1, raises 0 under copy-in, copy-out and 1 in place, though it passes [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout) without its receiver clause (`receiver_clause_needed`).

## Where the model departs from the Reference

- **`io` and capabilities**: of the natives that have a capability ([facts.capability-natives](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.capability-natives)), the calculus has those of `console` alone: `print`, `eprint`, and `std.io`'s `write`, `ewrite`, `read_line`, `poll_line` and `read_all`, whose rows hold `io` (`HasType.op`); `io.flush()` is `()` ([Executable semantics](#executable-semantics)). It has no native of `std.fs`, `std.net`, `std.clock`, `std.cli`, `std.random` or `std.process`, and no `exit`, the native that performs `io` with no capability; `task.spawn(f)` and `s.spawn(f)` are the call of `f` through a binding ([Shared values, tasks and parallel calls in the calculus](#shared-values-tasks-and-parallel-calls-in-the-calculus)), and `async`, which they perform, holds `io` in the calculus. So [facts.io-and-capabilities](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.io-and-capabilities) is stated for the natives of `console`: code whose row lacks `io` reaches none of them (`Program.WellTyped.quiet`). The capabilities as facts ([facts.capabilities](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.capabilities)), the `without` limits that name them, grants and the facts of a package's own code ([facts.capability-grants](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.capability-grants), [facts.own-capabilities](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.own-capabilities)), and `unsafe`, which applies to C code the calculus doesn't have ([c.without-unsafe](../reference/20-builds.md#c.without-unsafe)), are left out. A row in the calculus names no capability, so the rule that `fs` and the others are names of row variables there ([facts.spelling](../reference/15-facts.md#facts.spelling)) is the parser's.
- **A generic record or variant type** is declared once, with its type parameters, and each use names it at its arguments ([declarations.type-parameters](../reference/10-declarations.md#declarations.type-parameters) and [declarations.type-arguments](../reference/10-declarations.md#declarations.type-arguments)): a literal and a constructor carry them, as every use of a generic function carries its instantiation, since the calculus has no inference ([types.no-type-arguments](../reference/07-types.md#types.no-type-arguments)). A field's or payload's type is its declared type at the arguments, and in a generic function's body an argument may be one of its own type parameters, rigid there. A value of an instance has the run-time arguments among its run-time type, so its fields have their types at them. Its fields and payloads hold no row variable, as for a type without parameters. A record that a handler is has no type parameters. Two parts are conservative: a value of a record or variant type at its arguments is taken to hold what each argument holds, whether a field holds one or not ([Shared values, tasks and parallel calls in the calculus](#shared-values-tasks-and-parallel-calls-in-the-calculus)), and a variant at a type parameter may hold a view in the calculus of windows. A record or variant type at its arguments is a key type of maps and sets when its fields or payloads at them are, and, conservatively, when its arguments are too, which [traits.structural-impls](../reference/11-traits.md#traits.structural-impls) doesn't ask of an argument that no field or payload holds.
- **A `for` loop** iterates the value its iterable had when the loop started, in both models of memory, as copy-on-write makes it do ([modes.change-while-iterating](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.change-while-iterating)). So E0404 rules out a hidden copy rather than a difference in meaning, and `InPlaceAgreement` rests on the other violation of [modes.overlapping-inout](../reference/13-modes.md#modes.overlapping-inout), overlapping `inout` arguments and `inout self` receivers.
- **Views** are in the core calculus as the reference interpreter holds them and as windows ([Views in the calculus](#views-in-the-calculus)), without `view.get`, which returns an `Option`, `view.chars` and `view.bytes`, iterators ([views.making-iterator](../reference/13-modes.md#views.making-iterator), [views.adapters](../reference/13-modes.md#views.adapters) and [views.consumers](../reference/13-modes.md#views.consumers)), and `show` and the tiers ([views.show](../reference/13-modes.md#views.show)). Indexing reads through a view as `view.get` does, panicking with `bounds` where it gives `None`. The calculus of views (`Stilt.Views`) has lists only, without calls, closures or iterators.
- **Projections** are forms of the calculus that mean calls, as the export writes them ([Projections in the calculus](#projections-in-the-calculus)), so the theorems cover them through those calls. The check of E0424 reads a branch as ending its paths only when it ends them by its form, a `return`, a `raise`, a panic, a `break` or a `continue`, not by its type `Never` alone, as a call of a function whose result is `Never` does, and allows a `yield` only as a statement of the body, its blocks and its branches, not in an operand, an argument, a `when`'s or a `with`'s block or a `try`'s operand, so it rejects more than the Reference. E0427 is checked as its consequence, that the body raises nothing of its own outside the operand of a `catch`, since the projection's function has the row of its access too; E0426 doesn't look through `Trait.m`; E0307 on a trait's projection, a `yield` of a view that isn't a place, and a projection's call as a projection's argument are left out. The promise of E0424 is of copy-in, copy-out, which in-place mutation and windows show the same as, and says nothing of a path that panics, which ends the run. The projection's function has its access's row too, and the model doesn't state that a `without` on the projection leaves the access's effects out of what it limits ([projections.raising-during-access](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.raising-during-access)).
- **Views that write** are calls that take their source `inout` ([Views in the calculus](#views-in-the-calculus)). In copies the scope of a writable binding holds a copy of the whole source, of which it writes only the part, where the reference interpreter copies the part alone; no run tells them apart, since the scope reaches the source only through the part. The two parts of `view.split` are copies in every model, written back when its function returns or raises, since two writable bindings of one source would be two `inout` arguments of one place, which the law of exclusivity rejects. E0429 is checked as no mention, in the scope's closure, of the variable the source is rooted in, so it also rejects reaching a part of that variable that the view doesn't write, which [projections.reaching-written-source](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.reaching-written-source) accepts; and no promise rests on it, since the closure reaches the other variables by copy. E0428 is checked of `var` bindings, not of a function's result that reads an `inout` parameter ([projections.results-that-write](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.results-that-write)). Left out: views assigned to the whole of a writable binding, a jump out of a writable binding's scope, the modes of [projections.writable-modes](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.writable-modes) and `show` ([projections.show](../reference/13-modes.md#projections.show)).
